=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.98)
E NC L OS U RE
JAPANESE TAXATION OF ALIENS
1. No Japanese taxation should discriminate against aliens.
2. No capital levy should be levied by the Japanese author-
ities in respect to property in or outside Japan owned by United
Nations nationals. Any capital levy on corporations and unincorporated
associations should be so devised as to exempt from the effects of
the levy, the shares and interests in the said corporations and uninoor-
porated associations held by United Nations nationals. If in the opinion
of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, exceptional circumstances
justify the imposition of any such capital levy, he should refer the
matter for consideration of the Far Eastern Commission.
FEC-055/4

provide that one of the functions of the Commission should be to
"formulate the policies, principles and standards in conformity
with which the fulfillment by Japan of its obligations under the
Terms of Surrender may be accomplished."
It is further provided that when such decisions are
made by the Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government
shall prepare directives in accordance with the policy decisions
of the Commission and shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander
through the appropriate United States Government agency."
74o..
At the twentieth meeting of the Far Eastern Commission
held at 2516 Massachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington, D.C., on
18 July 1946, the enclosed policy decision relative to Japanese
Taxation of Aliens, was unanimously approved.
As Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I
have been instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of
JUL 251946
the Commission, in order that the appropriate directives may be
prepared and transmitted to the Supreme Commander in accordance with
the Terms of Reference.
In approving the statement of polioy, the Commission
recommended that in connection with this policy decision, the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers should be informed that the Far
Eastern Commission has currently under consideration additional
policy with respect to extraordinary taxation designed to meet war
costs or other charges falling on the Japanese as a result of the
war, such as the Increased Property Tax Law and the War Profits Tax
Law as Imposed on Juridical Persons.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
RESTRICTED
DATE: August 1, 1946
SUBJECT:
Exemption of United Nations Nationals from Certain Internal
Extraordinary Tax LMeasures in Japan.
PARTiciPAnTs: Mr. Martin, Mr. Whitman, Mr. Barnett--JK; Mr. Freeman--Le;
Mr. Arnold--CP.
894.512/8-146
COPIES TO:
CP - Mr. Gay
ITP
JK - Mr. Whitman
A meeting was held this afternoon in Mr. Martin's
office to consider the position of the U.S. member of the
Far astern Cormission in connection with the above subject.
ir. Martin stated that the FEC is considering proposals
to exempt United ations nationals and their property in
FILED
Japan from three scecific Ja unese intornal tax measures
constitutin extraordinary taxation designed to meet
CS
Japanese War costs. The taxes are a Capital Lovy, an
Excess War Profits tax, and an Increased Property tax.
NV
Initial ly, a majority of the FEC favored exemption of UN
nationals from all three taxes, in opposition to the United
States position of equal treatment to UN and Japanese
nationals alike. On the grounds that it was a more excessive
tax and considerably different from the other two, the U.S.
conceded granting the exemption in the case of the capital
levy only, in return for which the British agreed tentative-
ly to equal treatment, (no UN exemption) on the other two
894.512
taxes. Later, however, the British members stated they had
received new instructions supporting their former position
on the rounds that these taxes were extraordinary taxes
designed to meet Japanese war costs, that the Japanese had
brought the war on themselves and hence should pay these
particular war taxes themselves. The U.S. still continued
na,
According
Rev.
Uat.
Dift.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
2
According to Mr. Freeman, who is speaking for the U.S.
on the FEC committee considering this question, two main
arguments have been presented for the U.S. position: 1-
That the question must be regarded from the long-range
point of view, that our policy in such matters elsewhere
is to favor national treatment (i.e., no discrimination
for or against foreign nationals in domestic tax legislation),
and that to make an exception for Japan would be inconsistent
with that policy; 2- That these tax measures are designed
to aid in rehabilitating Japan, that UN nationals and their
property in Japan will benefit from rehabilitation, and
hence should share in t he cost. The U.S. stand is supported
at present only by the Philippines and the USSR.
In reply to a question from Mr. Martin, I stated that
CP's interest in the matter was to have a consistent foreign
commercial policy, that our position was substantially the
same as the point which Mr. Freeman had made (#1 in the
precedin paragraph); that an exception to the policy of
national treatment in the case of Japan would be hard to
justify in the light of our Proposals for World Trade and
Employment.
A meeting of the FEC committee on this subject will
be heId tomorrow, Friday, at 3:00 P.M. Mr. Freeman stated
that unless he could get some additional arguments for the
U.S. position, the committee would probably take the British
position of exemption from all three taxes and his only
alternative would be to file a minority report. I stated
I would try to et some arguments from our people and
give then to him. Mr. Martin suggested and Freeman agreed,
that CP shculd send a man to the meeting with Freeman, a
man to argue the U. S. position. I said I would pass that
suggestion on to CP and let them know by Friday noon.
It would seem that the CP representative should be someone
who kmows our commercial policy generally as regards
national treatment, particularly in taxation matters, and
who kmows how the subject is treated in our various commercial
treaties in order to answer possible questions from FEC
committee members.
my
2
CP:JArnold:dmr
8/1/46

MEMORANDUIA FOR THE SECRETARY, JOINT CHIEFS OF STAFF:
Subject: Directive Regarding Japanese Taxation
of Aliens.
There is enclosed a draft directive prepared on the
basis of a policy decision (also enclosed) unanimously ap-
proved at the twentieth meeting of the Far Eastern Commis-
sion on July 18, 19ló, under the provisions of paragraph
II, A, 1 of its terms of reference.
It is requested that the enclosed directive be for-
warded by the Joint Chiefs of Staff, if the directive in
their opinion satisfactorily expresses the policy decision
of the Far Eastern Commission, to General MacArthur for
his guidance in accordance with paragraph III, 1 of the
terms of reference of the Commission.
The Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission
requests that the Joint Chiefsof Staff, in transmitting
the enclosed draft directive to the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, inform him that in approving the state-
ment of policy on which the enclosed directive is based,
the Commission recommended that in connection with this
policy decision, the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
should be informed that the Far Eastern Cormission has cur-
rently under consideration additional policy with respect
to extraordinary taxation designed to meet war costs or
other charges falling on the Japanese as a result of the
war, such as the Increased Property Tax Law and the War
Profits Tax Law as Imposed on Juridical Persons.
For the State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee:

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
(copy)
DIRECTIVE REGARDING JAPANESE TAXATION OF ALIENS
The following statement of policy, adopted by the
Far Eastern Commission on 18 July 1946, under the pro-
vision of paragraph II, A, 1 of its Terms of Reference
has been received from the State, ar and Navy Departments
for transmission to you as a directive for your guidance
in accordance with paragraph III, 1, of those Terms of
Reference. You should take appropriate steps to insure
that any tax laws adopted by the Japanese conform to the
principles set forth in this statement:
"1. No Japanese taxation should discriminate
against aliens.
"2.
Ho capital levy should be levied by the Japanese
authorities in respect to property in or out-
side Japan owned by United Nations nationals.
Any capital levy on corporations and unincor-
porated associations should be so devised as to
exempt from the effects of the levy, the shares
and interests in the said corporations and unin-
corporated associations held by United Nations
Nationals. If in your opinion exceptional cir-
cumstances justify the imposition of any such capi-
tal levy, you should refer the matter for consider-
ation of the Far Eastern Commission."

My dear Mr. Secretary:
The memorandum of Assistant Secretary Hilldring to the
Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, dated 12 September
1946, transmitting for information and appropriate action the
message from the Supreme Commander with respect to the Commission's
proposal to release its consultation on the subject of Japanese
extraordinary tax laws to the press, has been received. The
original consultation of the Commission on this subject, and the
proposal for its release to the press, were contained in the
letter of the Chairman to you dated 28 August 1946, transmitting
the text of the dooument SC-0l6/8.
8
The reply of the Supreme Commander was submitted to the
Far Eastern Commission at its twenty-fifth meeting on 12 September
1946, and the Commission unanimously agreed to accept the recom-
mendation of the Supreme Commander that a press release be post-
CS/A
poned until action by the Japanese Diet on the tax bills has been
completed.
As Seoretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I
have been instructed to forward this decision of the Commission to
you, with the request that you inform the Supreme Commander accord-
ingly.
Sincerely yours,

DITISION OF JAPANESE AND KOREAN
TO
JK - Mr. Martin
ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
DATE: 12-10-46
DEBARTMENT OF STATE
pand 2-6-47 C.Jeoher
FROM
SUBJECT:
Application of Capital Levy Tax to Koreans Resident
in Japan.
894.512/1
The attached memorandum which Mr. Cho, a Korean seoretary
to Prince Ri submitted to Mr. William J. Sebald raises the
question as to the application of the capital tax levy on
Koreans resident in Japan.
Mr. Sebald has indicated that to his knowledge, SCAp has
not as yet determined the course that is to be taken in apply-
ing the levy to Koreans, but feels that this, and other questions
12-1046
related to the treatment whioh will be acoorded Koreans in Japan
may be referred to Washington for a decision.
Despite the faot that SCAP has been using every means to
induce Koreans to return to Korea, an estimated 40opoo remain.
In some instanoes, Koreans have considerable business interests
of their own in Japan, and others have managed to find prominent
positions in large Japanese business concerns. Black market
operations have been a fertile field in which Koreans have
participated heavily. It is estimated that a majority of
Koreans however, will probably fall within the exempt olassi-
fication of those having capital of no more than 30,o00 yen.
Should the capital levy tax be imposed upon Koreans, all
of Mr. Cho's arguments will doubtless find expregeten from
Koreans in Japan and in Korea as well.
In case Koreans are treated as United Nations nationals,
Mr. Sebald has indioated that it would constitute an open in-
CS/V
vitation for Koreans to engage in cloaking operations which
would be used by Japanese to escape the provisions of the tax.
It is realized that the problems raised by this issue are
similar to others concerned with the treatment of Korns in

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.98)
LEGATION OF SWITZERLAND
WASHINGTON 8. D. C.
MEMORANDUM
The Legation of Switzerland has been apprized of the
tenor of communications recently exchanged between the
Swiss Diplomatic Mission in Japan and the General Head-
quartere, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
894.512/3-2647
Diplomatic Section, Tokyo, in order to define the extent
of application of tax laws promulgated by the Imperial
Japanese Government. It was found that neither the Capital
Levy Law nor the War Indemnity Special Measures Laws provide
exemption from taxation by reason of the possession of the
nationality of a neutral country and that only nationals
of certain of the United Nations are exempted from taxation
as set forth in the Enforcement Regulations for these taxes.
CS/A
According to information supplied to the Swiss Diplo-
matic Representative in Tokyo, this discrimination against
594.512
nationals of neutral countries apparently originated in
Washington. The Legation of Switzerland has therefore been
instructed to approach the American authorities in this
question.
eg7

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
二2
The Swiss request for treatment under these tax laws
of Swiss nationals and firms on an equal basis with allied
nationals is primarily based on the most-favored-nation
provisions of Article 14 of the Swiss-Japanese Treaty of
Settlement of June 21, l9ll. This clause reads as follows
in its translation:
"The high contracting parties agree in all
matters relating to commerce and industry
that every privilege, favor, or exemption
which one of the parties extends or will
extend in the future to nationals of any
other foreign country will be extended
immediately and unconditionally to the other
contracting party, since it is their inten-
tion to treat in every respect commerce and
industry of both contracting countries on
the basis of most-favored nation."
Under the proposed Japanese tax laws, however, the
activity of Swiss firms in Japan must be considered jeopar-
dized and their very existence threatened by the application
of such regulations which seem contrary to the letter and

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
spirit of the Swiss-Japanese Treaty of Settlement. This
all the more since tax privileges have been accorded to
third countries.
The Swiss Government is unable to understand why
Swiss nationals and firms should be given a less favorable
treatment than those of other third countries. Such dis-
crimination can hardly be considered justified by
Switzerland in view of the provisions of Article 14 of the
Swiss-Japanese Treaty of 19ll. Furthermore, Switzerland,
as former Protecting Power for United States interests in
Japan and also for Japanese interests in certain belligerent
countries, would consider itself entitled to expect for its
nationals a treatment as favorable as that granted to
nationals of third countries.
The Legation would therefore appreciate it if the
competent American authorities would be disposed to use
their good offices with a view to obtaining that the
decision excluding Switzerland from these tax exemptions
be revised in the light of the above considerations.
March 26, 1947
R.400.2 Bp/md

Rtwre to KruI
n.S.
DATE: April 11, 1947
TO : A-H - K/S
RAn
FROM : JK - Mr. Whitman
sUBJECT: Applioability of Japanese Extraordinary Taxes to Swiss
Property in Japan
1. It is recommended that the memorandum of March 26, 1947 from
FW 894.512/3-2647
the Legation of Switserland, together with the reply of the Depert-
ment of State attached herewith, be transmitted to the U.S. repre-
sentative in the FEC, for the information of the FEC and such comments
or other action as the Commission may desire to take.
2. In respect to the present policies of the Japanese Government
under which the property of United Nations' nationals is exempted
from certain of the extraordinary tax measures recently promulgated
by the Japanese Diet, referenoe 1s made to SWNCC 281/4, 27 June 1946,
subject "Applicability of Proposed Japanese Extraordinary Tax Laws to
Non-Japanese Individuals and Corporations." Appendix "A" to Enclo-
sure "B" to that paper, indicates the background of the position taken
by SWNCC. The initial decision by SWNCC as contained in SWNCC 281/1,
was that this government had no objection to proposed Japanese extra-
ordinary tax laws being applicable to all non-Japanese individuals
and corporations. SCAP was so advised by JCS. When the matter was
taken up at FEC, however, Committee no. 6 of the FEC proposed on
May 9, 1946, that United Nations' property in Japan should not be
subject to a capital levy except with the prior consent of the FEC.
In difference to the position taken by Committee no. 6, SWNCC on
May 22, requested that JCS inform Gen. MacArthur that no action should
be taken with respect to the application of the Japanese extraordin-
ary taxes to United Nations' nationals until further notice. On
May 24, the Joint Economic Committees of the FEC endorsed the pro-
posed policy of Committee no. 6 with the U.S. alone dissenting. SWNCC
281/4 incorporated this FEC decision which was then transmitted to
CS/A
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.

MEMORANDUM

tenor of communications recently exchanged between the
Swiss Diplomatic Mission in Japan and the General Head-

Diplomatic Section, Tokyo, in order to define the extent
of application of tax laws promulgated by the Imperial
Japanese Government. It was found that neither the Capital
Levy Law nor the War Indemnity Special Measures Laws provide
exemption from taxation by reason of the possession of the
nationality of a neutral country and that only nationals
of certain of the United Nations are exempted from taxation
as set forth in the Enforcement Regulations for these taxes.
According to information supplied to the Swiss Diplo-
matic Representative in Tokyo, this discrimination against
nationals of neutral countries apparently originated in
Washington. The Legation of Switzerland has therefore been
instructed to approach the American autherities in this
question.

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The Swiss request for treatment under these tax laws
of Swiss nationals and firms on an equal basis with allied
nationals is primarily based on the most-favored-nation

My dear Mr. Secretary:
Koneral Hilldring's letter of June 4,
1947, enolosing a copy of the memorandum from the Legation of
Switserland, dated Maroh 26, 1947-requesting that the United
States Government use its good offices to obtain a revision ef
the polioy respeoting the application of certain Japanese taxes
to Swiss property in Japan.
The Far Eastern Commission has given sympathetio considera-
tion to the request presented by the Legation of Switserland on
behalf of its Government and has carefully weighed the reasons
set forth in the aforesaid memorandum with a view te supperting
its olaim. After due examination of all factors involved, the
Commission regrets that it cannot accede te the request ef the
Swiss Government. It believes that the natienals of neutral
countries, who were able to do business in Japan during the war
years and thus derived the benefits acoruing therefrom, should
continue to be subject to local laws, inoluding laws imposing
taxation, whioh have as their object the raising of revenue for
the vitally necessary economic rehabilitation of Japan.
The Far Eastern Commission considers that it does not fall
within the Terms of Reference of the Commission to determine the
offeot of the Swiss-Japanese Treaty of Settlement of June 2l, 19ll
S
in the present ciroumstances.
Sincerely yours,
Frann

I have the honor to enclose copy of a report concerning a meeting
of the Economic Study Council of the Asiatic Society held on January
15, 1948 at which the subject "Japanese Tax Reform" was discussed. As
may be gathered from the discussion (which is sunmarised at the be-
ginning of the report) delinquency in payment of Japanese taxes presents
an important problem in Government finance, It appeare that, if the
present rate of tax payment is not improved, the Japanese Government
must continue to finance its operations by inflationary deficit spending
methods. (From conversations with Japanese officials it appears that it
is impossible in any case to avoid some deficit spending in view of the
low rate of production and the cost to the Japanese economy of the
Ocoupation, The achievement of an apparently "balanced budget" is said
to be a deception in as mnch as deficit spending contimues through the
Reconstruction Finance Corporation.)
There seemed to be general agreement among the participants in the
discussion that present Japanese corporate tax rates are too high end
should be altered. Mr. H. SHAVELL of the Finance Division of the
Economic and Scientific Section of this Headquarters also recommended
establishment of a more productive local tax system. In addition he
was obviously disturbed that tax colleotors, who feel that tax rates
are mmoh too high, have taken it upon themselves to make decisions con-
cerming each individual's ability to pay. He mssorted that the Tax Re-
form Law was demooratically passed in the Diet and that any change to
be made therein is a respensibility of the Diet. This statement caused
CONTIDETIAL
emsement

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.96)
Tokyots 91
Feb. 12,1948
COMFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
amasement on the part of his listeners since it is commonly believed
that this Meadquarters' recommendations are used by the Government
to fores mest reform legislation through the Diet. It is unlikely,
therefore, that popular dissatisfaction could lead to a statutory
change not favored by advisers of this Headquarters.
Mr. Shavell strongly criticized corporations such as the Tokyo
Shibamra Electric Company which have misapuropriated taxes withheld
from employees' salaries. He stated that withheld taxes were in ar-
rears by 14.3 billion yen by November 1947.
As an example of this Headquarters' desire to encourage payment
2/
of taxes, there are enclosed five copies of an official Press Release
issued on January 21, 1948 in which Nr. Shavell praises the Japanese
Ministry of Pinance for initiating stringent measures against corpora-
tions which have misappropriated Government tax funde.

Summary Mr. Shavell described the revision made in
the Japanose tax system in Maroh, l947. He feels that
corporation tax rates should be lowered and that local tax-
ation methods should be placed on a more productive basis.
He also described the present high rate of delinquency
in tax payment whioh he attributed to the unfamiliarity of
the public with self-aseessment of income taxes and opposition
to the colleotion of high taxes on the part of tax collectors'
unions. Mr. Ikeda attributed tax delinquenoy to the failure of
the first trial of self-assessment, inefficienoy of collection
agencies, and deterioration of public conscience. Me related
the importance of complete tax colleotion to the solution of
the problem of deficit finance and its corresponding effect
upon the rehabilitation of the Japanese economy.
Mre Tokushima

Kr. Tokushime criticized tax legielation as a mmss of

priations for tax collecting expenses. He characterised present
tex rates as exorbitant and asserted that, in order to avoid
throwing busines men into bankruptcy, tax collectore have taken

Mr. Crane stated that high tax rates are discouraging

conjunction with new wge standsrds and low official
rices. leave busines men no profits.

an? other tax collectore for taking the liberty of determining

In response to e question Mr. Ikeda stated that it is

nationals" (a Japanese phrase commonly used to describe Chinese
and Eoreans) who have rafused to pay all taxes on the basis of

End of Summary.
The Chairman of tho Asiatic Society Economic Study Council, Dr.
AYUSawA Iwao presided at the meeting end the Secretary of the Council.
Hr. J. R. HanOLD presented the first speaker, Er. SHAV LL, who intro-
duced the subject for diacusston, "Js enese Tax Reform".
Hr. Shavell: The Japanese tax mystem up to March 194? was besed
upon a tax law of April 194o which chanzed tex laws of the Meiji period
as follows:
One, income tax waa given great weight; two, a second
corporetion tax replaced the corporation income tax; three,
local tax powers were diminished; four, the system of re-
distribution of taxes by the National Government to the
local Govemments was strengthened.
The physical inedequacies of the tax system, as revised in l940
beceme evident following the end of the wer. Incone from monopolies
of the Jenaneso Government failed to reep up with expenditures. In-
flation resulted in discrimination ageinst salaried worker since their
wagee were immediately eubject to taxation whereas other tax payere had
a period of from seven monthe to a year before payment was due (a deley
making poesible peyment in inflated currency). Furthermore, assessment
of incomee wes fraught with collusion because locally elected committees,
whose members were sponsored by vested interests, were often subject to
bribery.
Therefore.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.97)
Encl.1 to
Tokyo's 91
reb.12,1948
CONFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
Therefore, a reform tax law was passed in March 1947 and became
effective in April l947. Under this law the following changes were
made: self-assessment was substituted for governmental assessments,
a single progressive income scale replaced varied income tax
schedules; a corporate income tax was added; the excessive profits
tax was lowered; luury taxes were increased; and taxes on necessities
were lowered.
However, Jananese tax collection is far behind schedule. Taxes
at prcment uncollected amount to 94,000,000,000. Some of the reasons
for tax delinquencies srem to be laxity of governmont administration,
ignorance on the part of the public, and labor unrest in tax offices.
For exemple, tax collectors will not prosecute tax delinquents and
collect only small sums as penalties. In addition they will not attach
and sel! property belonging to delinquents and, despite the number of
delinquencies, there has been in all- Jepan not one person convicted of
tax evasion. The fact thet esrly tax publicity was poor is not a suf-
ficient excuse for the failure of the public to accept responsibility
for tex peyment. With regard to labor unrest, tax employees have re-
sorted to  slow-down campaign. Assessment and re-assessment notices
have not been despatched, on the grounds that employees are not paid
a "living wage". Placards hive been posted in tax offices informing
the public that it should not comply with such an "inoquitable tax
syatem". However, labor obstruction has been remedied in part and
therc are few such cases at present.
Under the new tax law, administrative measures heve been improved.
Thore is broeder selection and a single progressive income tax schedule.
Some heve criticized as premeture the adoption of the self-assessment
method of tex reporting but retention of "disinterested" local assess-
ment committees would have been a continuing source of injustice. Bov-
ever, it will take from five to ten yeers to fudge the success of the
new method end in the interim universal government re-assessments will
be necessery.
There anpears to be little need for further statutory change except
for the addition of a corporation tax to be levie'd by the Mational Goverm-
ment, end employment of more productive local taxes. Corporation taxes at
present are discriminatory, as they are levied on the basis of a ratie
of dead earnings to total invested capital. In addition corporation tares
are exorbitant and prevent industrial recovery. Another objectionable
feature is that the Wational Government now carries the burden of 60 per
cent of the cost of local finances. A real property tax offers one field
for additional local taxation although there is a grest deal of opposition
to such a tax. There can also be further administrative reform since
reforms on paper have not been effected in practice. For example, on ao-
count of lex enforcement measures, arrears are equal to budgetary defi-
ciencies.
Mr. Ikeda: (Mr. Kano translated). The greatest problem of the
Japanese Government at present is rehebilitation of the Japanese ecomomy:
a sound finance must be established if that problem is to be solved. To
that cnd it is necessary to balance the budget by seeking a mure souree
of revenue.

SUBJECT:
Report Concerning Discussion of "Japanese Tax Roform"
at a Meeting of the Economic Study Council of the
Asiatic Society, January 15, 1948.
PLACE:
Tokyo Correspondents Club.
PARTICIPANTS:
Dr, AYUSAWA Iwae, Secretary-General, Office of the
Central Labor Committee, Chairman of the Ecomemie
Study Council, Asiatic Society.
Mr. J. R. HAROLD, official of the Labor Divisien,
Economic and Scientific Section, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, Secretary of the Econemie
Study Council, Asiatic Society.

the International Bank and adviser to the Yokohama
Specie Bank.
Mr. Burton CRaNE, Hew York Times correspondent.
Summary. Mr. Shavell described the revision made in
the Japanese tax system in March, 1947. He feels that
corporation tax rates should be lowered end that local tax-
ation methods should be placed on a more productive basis.
He also described the present high rate of delinqueney
in tax payment which he attributed to the unfamiliarity of
the public with self-assessment of income tares and opposition
to the collection of high taxes on the part of tar collectorst
unions. Mr. Ikeda attributed tax delinqueney to the failure of
the first trial of self-assessment, inefficiency of collection
agencies, and deterioration of public conscience. He related
the importance of complete tax collection to the solution of
the problem of deficit finance and its corresponding effect
upon the rehabilitation of the Japanese economy.
Mra Tokushimm

Febraary 12,1948

CONFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
Mr. Tokushima criticised tax legislation as a mass of
patch-work changes and complained of insufficient appre-
priations for tar collecting expenses. He characterized present
tax rates as exorbitant and asserted that, in order to avoid
throwing business men into bankruptcy, tax collectors have taken
it upon themselves to determine individual ability to pay.
Mr. Crane stated that high tax rates are discouraging
legitimate business. He feels that tax levels, taken in
conjunction with new wage standards and low official
prices, leave business men no profits.
Mr. Shavell described as a major factor in tax delinquency
the misappropriation by certain corporations of taxes withheld
from employees' salaries. He strongly censured Mr. Tokushime
and other tax collectors for taking the liberty of determiming
the individual taxpayer's ability to pay.
In response to a question Mr. Ikeda stated that it is
physically impossible to collect taxes from "third country
nationals" (a Japanese phrase commonly used to describe Chimese
and Koreans) who have refused to pay all taxes on the basis of
the precedent set by exemption of Allied nationals from the mon
recurring capital levy.
End of Summery.
The Chairman of the Asiatic Society Economic Study Council, Dr.
AYUSAWA Iwao presided at the meeting and the Secretary of the Council.
Mr. J. R. HAROLD presented the first speaker, Mr. SHAVELL, who intro-
duced the subject for discussion, "Japanese Tax Reform".
Hr. Shavell: The Japanese tax system up to March 1947 was based
upon a tax law of April 1940 which chenged tex laws of the Neiji period
as follows:
One, income tar was given great weight; two, a second
corporetion tax replaced the corporation income tax; three,
local tax powers were diminished; four, the system of re-
distribution of taxes by the National Government to the
local Governments was strengthened.
The physical inadequacies of the tax system, as revised in 1940
became evident following the end of the war. Income from monopolies
of the Japanese Government failed to keep up with expenditures. In
flation resulted in discrimination against salaried workers since their
wages were immediately subject to taxation whereas other tar payers had
a period of from seven months to a year before payment was due (a delay
making possible payment in inflated currency). Furthermore, assessment
of incomes was fraught with collusion because locally elected committees,
whose members were sponsored by vested interests, were often subject to
bribery.
Therefore,
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.98)
mel,1 to
Tekyoss 91
Feb.12,1948
3
CONFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
Therefore, a reform tax law was passed in March 1947 and beceme
offective in April 1947. Under this law the following changes were
made; self-assessment was substituted for governmental assessments.
a single progressive income scale replaced varied income tax
schedules; a corporate income tax was added; the excessive profits
tax was lowered; luury taxes were increased; and taxes on necessities
were lowered.
However, Japanese tax collection is far behind schedule. Taxes
at present uncollected amount to 94,000,000,000. Some of the reasems
for tax delinquencies seem to be laxity of government administration.
1gnorance on the part of the public, and labor unrest im tax offiees.
For example, tax collectors will not prosecute tax delinquents and
collect only small sums as penalties. In addition they will mot attash
and sell property belonging to delinquents and, despite the mumber of
delinquencies, there has been in all Japan not one person convieted ef
tax evasion. The fact that early tax publicity was poor is not a saf-
ficient excuse for the failure of the public to accept responsibility
for tax payment. With regard to labor unrest, tax employees have rew
sorted to a slow-down campaign. Assessment and re-assessment notiees
have not been despatched, on the grounds that employees are not paid
a "living wage". Placards have been posted in tax offices informing
the public that it should not comply with such an "inequitable tax
system". However, labor obstruction has been remedied in part and
there are few such cases at present.
Under the new tax law, administrative measures have been improved.
There is broader selection and a single progressive income tax schedule.
Some have criticised as premature the adoption of the self-assessment
method of tax reporting but retention of "disinterested" lecal assess
ment committees would have been a continuing source of injustice. Hew-
ever, it will take from five to ten years to fudge the suoeess of the
new method and in the interim universal government re-assessments will
be necessary.
There appears to be little need for further statutory change ezeept
for the addition of a corporation tax to be levied by the National Gevern-
ment, and employment of more productive local taxes. Corporation taxes at
present are discriminatory, as they are levied on the basis of a rasie
of dead earnings to total invested capital. In addition corporatiom taxes
are exorbitant and prevent industrial recovery. Another objectionable
feature is that the National Government now carries the burden of 60 per
cent of the cost of local finances, A real property tax offers one field
for additional local taxation although there is a great deal of opposition
to such a tax. There can also be further administrative reform since
reforms on paper have not been effected in practice. For example, on ao-
count of lax enforcement measures, arrears are equal to budgetary defi-
ciencies.
Hr. Ikedai (Mr. Kano translated). The greatest problem of the
Japanese Government at present is rehabilitation of the Japanese ecomomy:
a sound finance must be established if that problem is to be solved. To
that end it is necessary to balance the budget by seeking a sure souree
of revenue.
Upon

The Government is doing its utmost to balance the budget but healtky
finance depends uvon full collection of anticipated tax revenues. However,
tax collection has been exceedingly unsatisfactory and delinquency at the
end of the period from April to December 1947 amounted to 10,000,000,000.
One of the reasons for such delinquency is the failure of the first
triel of self-assessment which has been only twenty percent of Govermment
expectations. In addition delinquencies are increasing, partly on account
of the inefficiency of collection agencies and partly because of the
deterioration of public conscience.
The present tax system has presented upprecedented problems. It is
of primary importance to publicize the necessity for tax paymeat. Ome of
the problems of the future is rationalisation of the tax system sincs an
increase in yen income does not mean corresponding increase in real earm
ings. In addition adjustments in family allowances are necessary and
production must be fostered to increase revenues from indirect tazes.
Dra Ayuseye: The third speaker is Mr. TOKUSHINA Yonesabure, Vice
President of the Tax Collectors Union.
Thirty thousand tax collectors are employed by the Finance Winistry,
twenty-three percent of whom aro girls paid on the basis of the eighteen
hundred yen monthly base wage level. The Tax Collectors Union is said to
have a membership of 4,500.
Hre Tokushime (Mr. Kano interpreted): I agree with Mr. Ikoda that
Japanese tax revenues are in a critical position. This is because of
patch-work changes resulting from superficial thinking on the part of
Government officials and members of the Diet. In the book "Japan's
Finances" written before the war, Dr. Molton said that the expense of
collecting taxes in Japan was very small in comparison to army and navy
erpenditures, so small that efficient collection would seem impossible.
The Japanese people, like other Asiatic people, do not understand the
meaning of love or duty, or conscience. That people should obey without
understanding is accepted practice in Japan but it is impossible to obtain
success in tax revision without first building a foundation in the minds of
the people. Because of the Japanese habit of submitting all matters for
decision to Government officials it is difficult to awaken people to the
real meaning of taxation.
At presens it is the honest people who suffer, and who, on account
of correct assessment of their taxes, must sell their belongings so that
they can pay. Nevertheless, tax evaders are comfortable. I know of
several individuals in Tokyo who earn between sixty and one hundred
million

Wage and salary earners face great difficulties. According to
investigations, persons earning 4,200 yen a month pay 15.9 pereeat ef
their wages as taxes and persons earning 3,600 yen per month pay 13.6
percent as taxes. A laborer in the United States (with a wife and child)
earning $50 a week pays only 3.8 percent of his wages in taxes. Japamese
laborers cannot live on their wages and must sell property to make up a
deficiency amounting to between five and six handred yen a month. Unless
justice is achieved the people will become extremely dissatisfied becaase
they see that the new-rich evade taxes. In Japan enterprisers do net
correctly assess their incomes and, because there is no public accountant
system, the whole burden of assessment falls upon the tax collectors.
Since collectors are now requiring payment in accordance with their owm
estimates as to individual ability to pay, a reduction in the rate of
taxation would not lessen revenue. In any event, revenues cannot be in
creased until persons are found who can pay increased taxes. However,
union members are doing their utmost to insure increased collections and
to fight inflation. The tax system should be revised, inspection of tax
payerst books by tax collectors should be legalized and greater funds
should be allotted for tax collection purposes.
The meeting was then opened for general discussion by Mr. Burton
CRANE, New York Times correspondent, who asserted that double taxatiom
of corporate income makes business revival in Japan impossible. In the
United States, he said, there is 495 percent double taxation in the lowest
brackets whereas in Japan there is 68 percent double taxation in the
lowest brackets. He feels that this encourages black market loans in un-
recorded business deals in an effort to avoid taxation. For that reason
he asked if more reasonable tax would not provide greater revenue. Mr.
Shevell agreed that it would and stated that the Government would gain by
basing the corporation tax system on invested capital from whatever souree
obtained. Some taxes giving small returns might be abolished.
Mr. Shavell noted that fermers have been exempt from withholding
taxes and will be required to pay taxes under the new tax law for the
first time this month. The sum of eighteen billion yen collected up to
the present represents a tax paid only by wege earners.
Mr. Shavell pointed out that a large amount of taxes withheld from
wage earners' salaries have been misappropriated by employers and cited
the Tokyo Shibaura Electric Company which owes more than one hundred
million yen to the Government, representing taxes already collected from
employees. He pointed out that up to November 1947 withheld taxes were in
arrears by l4.3 billion yen and that such arrears are expected to amount
to thirty to forty billion yen. He feels that the Government must punish
large corporations which, because of their financial difficulties, mis-
appropriate the taxes they collect.

ALERT UNIVERSITY PROFESSORS NEEDED TO FOSTER
ACADEMIC FREEDOM IN JAPAN. SCAP OFFICIAL DECLARES
A vigilant national organization of university professors in
Japan--with strong local chapters--will provide this country with
one of its best protections for academic freedom, a SCAp education
official declared today in commenting on the current membership
drive of the University Professors' Association of Japan.
The Association, organized since the war and now more than
2,500 strong, is seeking to enlist all qualified professors in
Jepan as members. Professors, assistant professors and lecturers
in accredited institutions, with more than two years' teaching
experience, are eligible to join. Forty-three colleges and univer-
sities are now represented in the Association.
"Japan offers a ripe field for such an organization," the
SCAP official said. "The barriers formerly imposed on the
universities by the official Shinto mythology and militaristic
government, severely handicapped the academic ideal of freedom of
teaching and research.
"No broad research in archeology, anthropology, history,
government, economics or sociology could be carried to its ultimate
conclusions in public print because academic liberty was stifled
in Japan."
The SCAP official said the battle for this freedom still
is being fought in many countries. "Japan," he added,"with the
unusual advantage of having a specific constitutional reference to
academic freedom is legally ahead of other countries where
acquisition of this freedom has taken years of struggle."
A strongly-worded resolution was passed by the Association
recently calling for establishment of faculty meetings, or
councils, to give professors some voice in school administration,
the official said. This resolution, which implements academic
freedom requirements of the School Éducation Law, promises
"appropriate action" against institutions which show "flagrant
disregard" for rights of professors to voice their opinions in
administrative matters involving their teaching and research.
Dr. Shigeru Nambara, president of Tokyo University, is
president of the professors' association.

SCAP TAXATION OFFICIAL COMENDS DRIVE AGAINST
TAX DELINQUENT CORPORATIONS
A SCAP taxation official today commended the Japanese Ministry
of Finance for initiating a nationwide "crackdown" on corporatior
who have misappropriated government tax funds.
(more)
(over)

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
Henry Shavell, taxation advisor, Finance Division
Economic and Scientific Section, said the drive, aimed at
payers delinquent one million yen or more, will affeet 450
corporations who owe the government approximately three billt
yen.
Describing the drive as the most sweeping campaign against
large tax delinquents ever undertaken in Japan, Mr. Shavell
praised the "swift government action" last Saturday in attaching
properties of 200 corporations in the Tokyo area.
Similar action was taken Monday against 63 corporations in
the Osaka area and action against the remainder of the corporatlons
will be taken by the end of the month, the SCAP official declared.
In addition, he said, the Japanese Government has ordered
the country's principal delinquent corporation, Tokyo Shibaura
Electric Co., to complete payment of 239.3 million yen in back
commodity,. income and other taxes by Feb. 15.
The Finance Ministry has further indicated it will attach
the company's property if the deadline 1s not met, and will.sell
at auction enough of its 30-odd subsidiaries to meet the tax bill,
Mr. Shavell said.
Production Will Not Be Impeded
In all cases, he added, the Japanese Government will initiate
attachment proceedings, giving a period of 30 to 60 days before
taking action to auction off corporate assets.
"Even though these measures may bring changes in ownership,
production will not be impeded," Ir. Shavell explained. "In
cases of essential. industries, the Finance linistry will insure
that operation is not affected during ownership changes. However,
no such considerations are plannad for night.clubs, movie houses
and other forms of non-essential industries."
:Mr. Shavell said corporations who have not peid: to the
government income taxes withheld from employees wages and commodity
tax receipts also'face a 36.5 per cent interest rate on such
taxes.in arrears.
"Such enforcement action may come as a shock to the public,
but it is taken as a metter of course in other countries," the
tax advisor said. "Such action is long overdue in  Japan."
He added that action against all tax delinquents must be
continued not only as a public duty and an anti-inflation
measure, but to help balance the national budget. He said if
the estimated 20 billion yen in tax delinquencies at the end of
Fecember had been collected, the rate of collections nearly.
would have attained the Ievel estimated in the budget of the
current fiscal year.
Mr. Shavell said corporations incurred their huge deficits
by not paying admission, commodity and personal income taxes
collected by the firms-but not paid to the treasury, making them
technically guilty of misappropriation of government funds.
In the case of the Tokyo Shibaura Electric Co., the Japanese
Government reported, the corporation used for its own business
120.2 million yen of income taxes collected from employees and
not turned over to the government, plus an additional 63.3
million yen in commodity taxes. Its total deficit of 239.3
million yen does not include 140 million yen of wer indemnity

ALERICAN MINE OFFICIAL ARRIVES TO ADVISE
JAPANESE ON MINE SAFETY CODE
Russell G. Warncke, United States Bureau of Mines safety
official, arrived in Tokyo by air yesterday from Washington to
act as advisor to Japanese officials on the formulation of a mine
safety code to help reduce the high fatality rate in Japanese
rines, SCAP's Natural Resources Section announced today.
Mr. Warncke, a mining engineer and mine inspector from the
office of the chief of the Health and Safety Division of the
Bureau, will serve in Japen for 90 days as consultant to the NRS
Mining and Geology Division.
He will advise the Ministry of Labor and the Ministry of
Commerce and Industry, which are now engaged in drafting a safety
code to provide an inspection service for Japanese metal and coal
mines.
In this task, Mr. Warncke will work with Charles C. Hicks,
assistant to the vice president  of the United Mine Workers of
America, who is serving in SCAP's Labor Division as a consultant
on coal mining problems.
An average of 20 men are killed and more then 1,100 are
injured weekly in Japanese mines, NRS officials said. "Besides
the loss of human life, the number of mine accidents in Japan
have seriously impaired coal production and adversely affected
the Japanese economy," they added.
Mr. Warncke will leave Tokyo Feb. 4 with a party of SCAP
officials for an inspection trip to the Hitachi copper mine in
Ibaragi prefecture and the Joban coal fields in Fukushima prefec-
ture. He later will visit coal mines in Hokkaido and Kyushu,
paying particular attention to faulty ventilation facilities which,
NKS officials say, have caused a number of mine explosions in this
country.
A graduate of the Pennsylvania State College School of Mines,
the consultant has served the U. S. Bureau of Mines for seven
years, administering a safety education program and seeking com-
pliance with federal safety codes in mines all over the U. S.
Previously, he was a mining engineer with a private concern in
New Mexico. Mr. Warncke's home is at 4812 Western Ave., Chevy
Chase, Md.

CAPTAIN MCINTYRE NAMED ASSISTANT FEC EDUCATION OFFICER
Capt. Niles J. McIntyre, Napa, Calif., has joined the GHQ
Troop Information and Education Section as assistant Theatre
Education Officer, it was announced today. A veteran of the
Asiatic-Pacific campaigns fought on Luzon, Philippine Islands,
Captain McIntyre was graduated from the Army Information School,
Carlisle Barracks, Pa., prior to coming to the Far East Command.
Entering the service at Fort Riley, Kans., in 1938, Captain
McIntyre was commissioned a second lieutenant upon completing
Officers Candidate School at Ft. Knox, Ky., in 1942. He has
served with the 9th Armored Division and the 82d Infantry Training
Battalion, and has had tours of duty as operations officer at
the Armored Replacement Center, Ft. Knox, and as legal officer with
the 25th Regimental Combat Team at Ft. Benning, Ga.

SCAP AUTHORIZES REESTABLISHMENT OF TELEGRAPH DEPOSIT SYSTEM
SCAP has cuthorized the Japanese Ministry of Communications
to re-establish its deposit system allowing payment of international
telegraph charges on a ronthly basis. The service, which will be
placed in operation immcdiately, is aveilable to business firms
and thc general public in Tokyo.
Indivicduals or organizations using this system will deposit
with the Ministry of Comrunications at the Tokyo Central
Telegraph Office a sum sufficient to cover telegraph charges for
the month. Deposits may be made in Military Payment Certificates,
Foreign Trade Pryment Certificates, British Armed Forces Special
Vouchers or Jrponese yen.
Each epositor will be furnished a book in which the
arount deposited is crecited. As telegrams are filed, charges
will be ceductec from the credit.
This system, which wrs widely used in Japan before the wer,
has certain advantages over the charge account system employed
by telegraph companies in the United States, CCS officiais noted.
It elirinates coilection procedures and the cccumulrtion of un-
collectable accounts.

NEW JUMP CLASS STARTS IN 1ITH AIRBORNE DIVISION
WITH THE FIGHTH ARLY IN CAMP CRAWFORD--A new.JumP ©las
composed of men who have volunteered to jump from a plane 
feet in the air, cruising along at a speed of about two miy
minute, opened this week at the llth Airborne Division trainia
center, Matsushima Army Air Base.
In the first week of training, the men will begin each day
with a brisk run and generous amounts of shaping-up exercises.
They also start classes to simulate actual jumping experiences.
Each man gets explicit instructions on each phase of jumping
before donning a chute and entering a C-46 for his first jump.
In the second week, training is put into practice With the
completion of five treining jumps the men gain their wings and
jump boot rights.
After returning to their originel outfits, these now qual-
ified troopers will begin jumping once every three months for
fifty dollars extra per month as jump pay, and may, intermittent-
ly, make a few tactical jumps.

SURVEILLANCE OF JAPANESE TAX ADMINISTRATION
1. The maintenance of adequate tax revenue by the Japanese
Government is at present a matter of vital conoern to the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers. To the extent that Japanese
Government expenditures are not financed with tax and other
revenues, the present inflation will be aggravated. With the
prospect that current fiscal year tax receipts may fall con-
s1derably behind budget estimates and thereby unleash added
1nflationary pressures, immediate action by the Japanese Govern-
ment with attendant active surveillance by military government
personnel becomes necessary. Instructions have been issued
relative to aditional surveillance teams to supnlement
military government units in this work.
2. Military government surveillance of Japanese tax
collections will be limited initially to the 450 local tax
offices of the national government, as l1sted 1n inclosure
2, and will not be extended at this time to cover tax offices
of municipalities or prefectures. Major emphasis should be
placed on the administration of the personal income tax,
as the potential revenue deficit in this tax is believed to
exceed that of all other national taxes combined. The ex-
tent of surveillance required and other information relevant
to tax surveillance are contained in inclosure 1 attached.
3. As tax collection goals and quotas have already been
distributed to the Tokyo, Nagoya, Osaka, Hiroshima, Takamatsu,
Kumamoto, Sannoro, and Senda1 Regional Financial Bureaus,
surveillance shouid be initiated as early as possible.. Monthly
reports as prescribed in inclosure 1 will'be submitted on
the last day of the month for January, February, Maroh, and
April 1948. It is desired that reports (Reports Control

IMPROVEMENT SEEN
IN TAX COLLECTIONS
SCAP Spokesman Says Police
Must See That Laws Are
Strictly Enforced
SCAP Labor and Finance Divi-
sion officials said yesterday a de-
finite improvement in Japan's tax
collection program has been noted
since the National Tax Collectors
Union issued specific instructions
to its locals.
Officials were informed that
members of local unions through-
out Japan were reminded by union
headquarters on January 20 that
their duty as responsible Govern-
ment officials is to collect and as-
sess taxes directly as prescribed by
law.
James S. Killen, Chief of the
Labor Division in ESS, in com-
mending the union for taking lead-
ership in the collection problem,
emphasized that success of the na-
tion's tax program in part depends
upon the courage and industry of
the individual collectors.
He emphasized the "need for
local procurators and police to see
that the tax laws are enforced and
that these officials give collectors
the kind of support needed to per-
form an effigient job."
ESS Finance Division offcials
recently returned from inspection
trips to Kyoto, Otsu, Osaka and
Kobe, where diffculty in tax cols
lections had been reported. They
said a major cause of controversy
in the inome tax collections has
been the feeling on the part of
many tax offcials and the public
that taxes should be paid. only in
accordance with the tax-payers
concept of his own ability, to pay.
Henry Shavell, tax specialist in
the Finance Division...in calling
attention. to the, January, S1 dead-
line for fling returns on income
earned in 1947, said that many
cases of under assesspent had been
noted. In such cases, Government
tax authorities expect to issue re-
assessments during the first 16 days
of February, he explaimed.
day grace periód,

Applicability of Taxes to Non-Japanese Nationals

2. There is no objection to the applicability of local and national
taxation to non-Japanese netionals except as specified in paregraph 3.
below, provided that such taxes are not discriminatory against non-Japamese
netionals. No tax, primerily designed to meet reparations or other oharges
falling upon the Japanese Government as a result of the war shall be imposon
by the Japanese Government upon United Nations nationals or their propertiom.

selaries of military personnel, of civilians attached to the ocupation
forces, end of accredited personnel of foreign diplometic missions, dologati
and other officiel representation atteched to the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powors. No tax besed upon or measurcd by income lawfully recoived

301 61 940
There is attachad a note from the Finnish
Legation dated October 13, 1948 regarding a claim
against the Government of Japan. The facts briefly
are these:
W894
In 194l a Finn bought some fishing nets from a
Jap firm. Delivery being prevented by the war, the nets
were stored and insured against war risk. During an
air raid they were destroyed. Insurance in the amount
of 5000 yen was paid the Swedish Legation (acting for
the Finn) and a certificate for balance due 445,000 yen
was issued. The latter was allegedly subsequantly
confiscated through a "Wartime Indemnity Tax" of the
Jap Government. It is alleged that this tax was not
applied uniformly to all foreign assets. The Finns claim
that there was therefore a violation of a m.f.n. provision,
applicable to taxation, contained in a Treaty of Commerce
between Finland and Japan. U.S. good offices are sought
in bringing the matter to the attention of the appropriate
Jap authorities to obtain settlement.
1348
Mr. Claxton of O, whom I consulted, has suggested
that a copy of the note should be appended to a lotter
addressed to r. Sebald in which he would be requested
8
to take such action as he deemed appropriate and to
report results to the Department.
Do you agree with this suggestion? If not I would
appreciate your views. Ir this course is appropriate
would NA draft? NOE will, of course, be glad to do so
if you prefer.
Attachment :
Note from the Finnish
Legation dated 10/13/48.
EUR:NOE:JOB:dah
4018

following:
Early in the year 1941 a Finnish businessman
Mr. William Metså purchased in Japan a quantity of fishing-
nets. The nets were fully paid for, but events later in
the said year prevented the shipment of these goods to
Finland. The fishing-nets were then stored at the warehouse
of the Japanese firm "The Miye Seimo Goshi Kaisha, Yokkaichi,"
CS/M
on behalf of the Finnish purchaser and insured against i. a.
war risk for 450,000 Yens.
When, in the fall of 1944, diplomatic relations
between Finland and Japan were severed, in accordance with the
Armistice Agreement between Finland and the Allied Powers,
Swedish Legation in Tokyo, being in charge of Finnish interest
His Excellency
The Homorable Rebert A. Levett
Asting Secretary of State
Engton, D. 6.

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.99)
2
in Japan, took care of the consignment referred to abeves
destroyed on the 18th of June 1945 and the Yokoham Specie Bank
according to the terms of the war risk insurance agreement, paid
5,00o Yens and gave to the Swedish Legation in Tokyo their interim
certificate for 445,000 Yens. This certificate was later exchanged
against a deposition certificate issued by the "Bank of Japan, in
accordance with the decrees covering war risk insurances, by which
decree the payments were frozen for a period of five years.
The Finnish purchaser was led to believe that the mmtter
of compensation was quite in order and that in due time he would
get the money, but by a letter dated August 6, 1947, from the firm
from whom the fishing-nets were purchased, the Finnish purchaser
was informed that:
.. . On the 15th July 1947, we wrote an enquiry
to The Japan Bank, Tokyo, to which, having had no reply,
we went up to Tokyo on 24th July to make enquiry. On the
25th, we approached The Japan Bank, where we were very sorry
to hear that the frozen deposit was already disposed as War-
time Indemmity Special Tax. As a fundamental principle, if
the application had been made by the 14th Dec., 1946, Yen
10.000.- for Company and Yen 50.000.- for Personality would
be excluded and the rest should be all paid as Wartime
Indemnity Special Tar. So, if the Swedish Legation hed net
applied on the 14th last December, you would have lef

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
to be paid. A few days ago, we received from the Japan
Bank a letter dated 29th July in reply to our enquiry. The
following are the key points of the letter.
#By the Insurance Paying Bank, the amount was deposited
under the tentative 5-year-frozen special deposit certifi-
cation of Japan Bank according to the Business Readjustment
Law. Later, this 5-year-frozen special deposit was abolished,
and the above deposit cert. of The J.B. was changed into the
Cert. of the Insurance Paying Bank. And, the Deposit was
put into national revenue as Wartime Special Tax Law except-
ing Yen 10,000 for Co., or Yen 50,000 for private personality
upon application. For foreign Firms or Private Personality
that have Nationality, if they are among those countries
written in the Article Ist of Property Tax Law, the Wartime
Indemmity Special Tax is not 1mposed. But, Sweden & Finland
are not found among such countries. So that, the deposit was
the object of Tax. For details, The Insurance Paying Bank, or
the 1st Section of National Tax, Tax Bureau of Financial
Department would reply upon enquiry."
The Finnish purchaser is of the opinion, and the Finnish
Authorities share his views, that the above described procedure
based on the provisions of The Wartime Special Tax Law, which Law
is understood to be a part of the internal Japanese legislation, is
not in accordance with the stipulations of the Treaty of Commerce
and Navigation between Finland and Japan, ratified on December 29, 1924
The Treaty, in Article I, Division 5, stipulates:
They (Finnish citizens) shall be at full liberty to
acquire and possess every deseription of property, movable

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.98)
一4
or immovable, which the laws of the country permit or shelt
permit the citizens or subjects of any other foreign country
to acquire and possess, subject always to the conditions
limitations prescribed in such laws. They shall be permitsed
laws of the country. They may dispose of the same by sale
exchange, gift, marriage, testament, or in any other manner,
under the same conditions which are or shall be established
with regard to native citizens or subjects. They shall alse
be permitted, on compliance with the laws of the country;
freely to export the proceeds of the sale of their property
and their goods in general without being subjected as
foreigners to other or higher duties than those to which
citizens or subjects of the country would be liable under
similar circumstances.*
And in Division 7:
*They (Finnish citizens) shall not be compelled to pay
taxes, fees, charges or contributions of any kind whatever,
other or higher than those which are or may be paid by native
citizens or subjects or the citizens or subjects of the most
favoured nation.
*It is understood, however, that the citizens or subjects
of the High Contracting Parties shall have to conform themselves,
in respect of the foregoing stipulations, to the special laws,
ordinances and regulations of the country which are generally
applicable to all foreigners alike.
The Finnish Authorities are also of the opinion that the
above-mentioned Treaty of Commerce and Mavigation has been and as
in full force and validity, notwithstanding the fact that di

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.98)
relations between Finland and Japan were, as stated,
of 1944; consequently, they feel that a citisen of Fimlam
Japanese legislation should be treated on the same basis as
of the most favored nation to the extent provided for in the
Commerce and Navigation.
The Finnish purchaser is further of the opinion that, as it
has been impossible for him to apply for the exemption of 50,000 Yens
within the time stipulated in The Wartime Special Tax Law because of
lack of communication facilities, he is at least entitled to payment
of the aforesaid amount as provided in the said Law.
Referring to the above and inasmuch as the Finnish Government
understand that the Japanese Government act under the guidance of the
Allied Council for Japan, I should feel extremely grateful if, through
Your Excellency's good offices, the viewpoints of the Finnish Government
should be brought to the knowledge of the Japanese Authorities concerned
and the decision of the Japanese Ministry of Finance in regard to the
applying of the Wartime Special Tax Law to the possible claims of
Finnish nationals and especially to the above-mentioned claim be adjusted
to conform with the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation between Pinland
and Japan.
Accept, Excellency, the renewed assurances of my highest
consideration.

To the

The Acting Seoretary of State transmits herewith a
note of October 13, 1948, from the Legation of Finland
pertaining to the application of Japanese tax measures
to certain Finnish property in Japan. The Acting Pelitisal
Adviser is requested to bring this matter to the attention
of the appropriate officials of General Headquarters with
a view to furnishing the Department with information to
serve as a basis of reply to the note.
In respect to the application of Japanese tax meas-
ures to foreign-owned property in Japan, it is the under-
standing of the Department that the offeot of the War
Indemnities Tax was substantially to cancel claims for
payment of war risk insurance, including claims evidensed
by certificates of the type referred to in the Legation's
note. It is further understood that in mscordanee with
the provisions of the War Indemnities Spesial Measures Law
Enforcement Regulations, exemptions from the application
of this law were granted to certain nationals or members
of the United Nations who are also exempt from the eapital
levy law. However, whereas the exemption of United Matiens
nationals from the capital levy law was in accordance with
a policy decision of the Far Eastern Commission (Peliey
Decision of July 18, 1946 on Japanese Taxation of Aliens),
it would appear that exemption of United Nations preperty
from the War Indemnities Tax was determined by the Supreme
OS/M
Commander pursuant to the responsibilities of the Supreme
Commander with respect to United Nations property in Japan.
For the information of the Acting Political Adviser
and for possible use in the consideration of the Finmish
request, there are also transmitted herewith copies of a

tax bureaus will be directly responsible to the new Tax Administra-

vision of their respective regional tax bureaus.
An indication as to the need for the reorganization of the
Japanese national tax administration is afforded by statements con-
tained in the following paragraph from a report entitled "General
Survey of Japanese Taxation," dated May 6, 1949, prepared by the In-
ternal Revenue Division, Economic and Scientific Section (Ess), Gen-

issued:
"One of the major objectives of tax administration re-
form is the reorganization of the Japanese national tax
system, which has a total personnel complement of approxi-
mately 64,000. This system is headed by the Taxation
Bureau of the Ministry of Finance with personnel numbering
approximately 250 individuals. Eleven regional bureaus are
responsible in their respective areas not only for tax col-
lections (approximately 59% of each bureau's personnel),
but also for all other duties of the Ministry of Finance,

A copy of the "General Survey of Japanese Taxation" is being
forwarded as enclosure to another despatch. Meanwhile, it will be
noted that, in accordance with SCAPIN 2OOl, the Japanese Government
is required to "provide tax personnel who are competent, qualified and
trained, insofar as possible, and adequate in number to administer
and enforce vigorously, but fairly, the tax laws of the Government."
In this connection, it may be noted that Mr. L. Harold MCSS, Chief,
Internal Revenue Division, ESS, reportedly stated at a press confer-
ence May 10 that "a government which permits an inefficient tax ad-
ministration to exist, thus fostering and encouraging unfair dis-
tribution of the tax burden among its citizens, is to be severely
censured." Mr. Moss is further quoted as stating, with specifilc ref-
erence to Japanese tax administration personnel, that "while there
are many able and conscientious tax officials...there are entirely
too many incompetent, inefficient, disloyal and even corrupt officials
in the tax service. These should be ruthlessly weeded out and replaced
with personnel of high moral and professional qualifications...immedi-
ately."
In order to attract qualified personnel, Mr. Moss reportedly recom-
mended the development of a career-merit personnel aystem and estab-
lishment among the people of the prestige and respect "which the tax
service justly deserves." Unless the Japanese Government assigns
priority to the problems of developing a truly professional tax ad-
ministration and enforcement systom, Mr. Moss reportedly warned, in-
equitable tax administration must inevitably continue.
While press reaction to issuance of SCAPIN 200l was limited, the
vernacular and Japanese-operated English language newspapers in Japan
have recently devoted much attention to the arrival here of the Shoup
Mission, the hope being freely expressed that the Mission would be
able, within the three or four months of its stay here, to effect
sweeping reforms in the Japanese taxation aystem with specific refer-
ence to reduction of taxes. A separate despatch on the Shoup Mission
is being prepared. Meanwhile, with final reference to SCAPIN 2001,
it may be noted that the Nippon Times. Tokyo, on May 13 reported that
the Cabinet on May 12 had decided to present to the Diet a bill to re-
vise the Finance Ministry Organization Law to provide for establish-
ment of the new Tax Administration Bureau. It was added that the new
Bureau, scheduled to begin functioning June 1, 1949, will consist of
four departments and ll local branch offices where revenue offices are
now situated. The 1l branches include Sapporo, Sendai, Tokyo (2), Osaka,
Hiroshima, Takamatau, Kumamoto, Nagoya, Fukuoka, and Kanazawa.

GENERL SURVEY OF J.P.NESE T.X.TION
1. Development of the Tax System. Prior to the twentieth century
taxation was characterized by exdessive dependence upon land taxes, with lis
concern for other revenue sources. It waa not until the turn of the century
that the tax system recognized the new forms of wealth created by urban coneenti
tion and the growth of industry and commerce. The personal income tax, although
first inaugurated in 1337, did not emerge as a significant producer of revenue
until the second decade of this century. During.1900-1930 the Japanese tax
aystem was developed in patchwork fashion, its major changes evolving chierly
as a result of emergency revenue demands created by wars with China and ussia
and by World War Is By 1930 the system had grown to fairly comprehensiv pro-
portions but was still overbalanced on the side of real-property taxation
2. During 1930-37 indirect consumption taxes rose to prominence. . Tobaceo,,
liquor, and commodity excises (including customs duties) aocounted for an averag
of 65 per cent of all tax revenues. Fiscal policy was framed in terms of low
taxes and deficit spend ing financed by borrowing. This reflected the Takahashi
financial plan for depression recovery. Mounting military expenditures neces-
sitated the reversel of this policy in 1937. With the North China affair-
developing into the "China affair," more and more "temporary emergency" taxee
were enacted to finance military expansion. s such temperary tax devices soon
proved fiscally inadequate, a more efficient mobilization of tax machinery was
seen to be indispensable to the realization of Japan's imperialistic ambitions.
This was the objeotive of the Tax Reform Law of pril, 1940.
3. The 1940 tax reform represented a milestone in the development of the
Japanese tax system. The income tax was given recognition, as the basic revenue
producer and emerged as the nucleus of the revised tax system. lthough pre-
viously applicable to both corporate and pensonal income, the income tax was now
restricted to personal levels, and a separato "corporation tax" was estebliahed
to tap corporate earnings. The l940 personal income tax was based essentially
upon the British and French income tax systems, since it embraced both a norm
tax (with rates differentiated as to source of incode) and a general surtax
all income. The newly oreeted conporation tax wes essentially

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.97)
texes on net imcome, capital steck, and surplus prooseds at
atsons.
4. Perhaps the most notable contribution of the 1940 reform was in th
field of national- and local-government tax integration. The Tax Reform Iw
allocated specific tax fields for the axclusive use of each lovel of governmont
Local-governmont echelons were forbidden to levy additions upon all but four
national taxes (the housc, land, business, and mining-right taxes). Maximm
levels of local-government surtaxes upon the latter taxes wero presoribed; The
national covernment acted also as a collection egeney for local governments. t
'redistributed dosignated tax receipts in their entirety to local govornments, as
well us fixed proportions of the qnnual revenue from tho income, corporation,
admission, and amuscment taxes. Redistribution was based upon prescribed
googrephical tax-equalization fornulas.
5. Post-Wer Developments. Major developnents in the Japanese Pax fiala
since V-J Day may bo summarized as follows:
a. Capital Levy and War Indomnity Tax Program:. In excraordinary tax
·progran, consisting of a capital levy and war indemnity special tax, was on-
acted in the fall of i94ó. The capital levy was a non-recurrent tax on privately
owned real and intangible property with ret-s graduated from ten to ninoty per-
cent. The tax was collectible by 15 Fobruary 1947. Latost cstinato of the yield
of the capital lovy approximates 40 billion yen comparod to the originally ebti-
mated yield of 43.5 billion jen. The war indemnity special tax provided for the
virtual cancellation of over lo0 billion yen in claims against the Japanese
Government arising fron the war and unpaid as cf 15 ugust 1945. Thc tax was
made sffective as of 14 Docember 1946. Certain corollary moasures designed to
eas. the impact of those two taxes upon the economy (special bankruptcy procoed-
ings, etc.) were also providud for.
b. Personal Income Tax of  pril 1947: A radical change in the statú-
tory form and administration of the Japanese personal inccno tex was mado offee-
tive 1 pril 1947.  current tax payment basis (roughly similer to the United
States practice) was adopted for the first time. Withholding at source. pre-
viously limited to normal tax liability on salary and wage income, was inoreased
in scope to cover full income tux liability; withholding wue oxtanded at a flat
rate of 20% to dividend and interest peyments.  single graduatod tax rate
structuro was mado applicable to ali forms.of income, replacing the earlie

and penalties, the basic tax law of 'pril 1947 is thet presently existing
(Soe p. 25 Appendix  for surrent rates).
c.
The Estate and Gift Tax: The estate and gift tax law was onaoted
in March 1947, and became effeqtive on 3 May 1947, simultaneously with abolition
of the earlier inheritance tax end the enforcement of the new Japanese Consti-
tution. Gift and astate components are entiroly separate and distinct, and are
 in no way integrated. Estete tax rates ere established in three differen't
scales based upon the rolctionship of heir and deceased and are gradueted from
10% to 65%. 4 basic oxemption of ¥ 50, 000 is pcrmitted. Charitable, educational
and scientific contributions are excluded from the tax base up to ten peroent of
the net value of the stace or y loo, ooo if smaller. Gift tax rates are gredu-
atcd from 15% to 65%, vith & busic lifetime exemption of ¥ 50, 000. Gifts valued
at less than  3, ooo pur donee per celendar year are excluded from the gift tax
base.
d. Trensition to "pay-as-you-go" income taxes: In sh ifting the personal
income tax to a current income basis a probiom erose concerning forgiveness of
1946 income tax liability due during 1947. Whereas 1946 normal income taxes on
earned income had been collected at source during 1946, all remaining tax
liability on 1946 incomes were still outstanding. The problem was solved by
the enactment of the Increased Income Tax ct of December 1946. This act, which
'provided for complete forgiveness of all 1946 tax liabilities due during 1947.
also provided for a one-time progressive tax upon substantial excesses of 1946
over 1945 personal income, exclusive of earned income. The tax mas imposed
during 4pril 1947, with rates graduated from 30% to 90%. The total yield of the
tax has approximated 9 billion yen.
e. Corporation Tax: The Japanese corporation tax has not as yet under-
gone comprehensive reforn. On V-J Dey, the tax embraced 4 tax elements: (1)
a normal net income tax of 35% applicable to domestio corporations, and 45%
applicable to foreign corporations; (2) an excess profits tax graduated from 10%
to 40% based upon current earnings over normal .period earnings or with respect
to invested capital; (3)  capital stock tax of 0.5% on capital stock and surplus
reserves; (4) liquidation proceeds tax (proceeds at liquidation or amalgamatipn

three tax components: (1) net income of all corporations, domestie and for

Correction of tendency towards preferential treatment of newly formed

In presenting this request, the Taxation Bureau of the Ministry of Finance was
asked to submit plans for these changes, or, as an elternative, to submit
evidence that the changes were unnecessary or undesirable. In the several con-
fereaces on this subject, the Taxation Bureau maintained quite consistently that
the corporation tax wus not impeding production nor discouraging capital invest-
mont, that the rates were well in line with the personal income tax rates, that
the accounting rules followed were well understood by businessmen, and that many
proprietorships vere incorporating.
Subsequont investigation revealed that the Taxation Bureau was substantially
correct; and that numerous complaints to GHe were probably based upon the
higher rate structure in effect the previous year. While the Taxation Bureau
never became interested in improving the application of the tax, it did develop
a vigorous interest in ono method of equalizing the application of exeess profits.
rates as between old and now companies. This method, the revaluation of fixed
assets, was advocated and almost demended. but no adequate supporting data was
ever presented. Following detailed consideration by several divisions of sCAP.
including Public Finance, Price Control and Distribution, and Inti-Trust and
Cartels, the proposal for revaluation was unanimously disapproved. The reasoning
behind this disapproval is fu lly explained in the files covering revisior of the
corporation tax.
The one result growing out of this work is a change in accounting rules under
which the premium on sales of stock will no longer be taxable.
