13. Reorganization of Tax System: One of the major objectives af
administration reform is thə reorganization of the Japanese national tax
which has a total personnel complement of approximately 64,000. This syst
is headed by the Taxation Bureau of the Ministr/ of Finance with personnel
numbering approximately .250 individuals. Eleven regional bureaus are responsi-
ble in their reapective areas not only for tax collections (approximately 55%
of ach bureau's personnel), but also for all other duties. of the Ministry of.
Finance, except the Monopoly Bureau. District tax offices, which are almoat.
completely independent of the regional bureaus and the Ministry of Finance,
now number 503. Thase district offices have very broad. practicelly autonomous
powers with regard to all matters releting to the assessment and colloction of
taxes. Thus, responsibility within tho existing organization is vury difficult
to place. Spocific plans for the reorganizatiou of the Taxetion Bureau have
been laid out for the Japanese Government in a recent directive (Sc PIN 2001.
4 May 49, Memorandum For Japanese Government, subjeot: Reorganization of
National Tax ministration of the Japanese Government, see p. 30, opendix ).
14. Present plans contemplace the establishment, on or about 1 June 1949.
of a national tax administration as a semi-autonomous agency of the Finance
Ministry. Under this proposal the regio al tax bureaus will be directly
responsible to the Tax Bareau, and the 503 district tax offices will be under
the supervision of their respective regional tax bureaus.
15. Prosecution of Tax Evaders: Enforcement of the tax laws has been
notoriously weuk. Not a single known case of conviction or punishment for
income tax evasion had ever been reccrded in Japan until November 1947. • When.
questioned on this point, Japanese officials replied that criminal punishment
for tax evasion was contrary to their philosophy of justice. Under 1946 and
earlier tax laws, furthermore, it would have been extremely difficult for the
Government to prove evasion or fraud. t any time prior to actual indictment,
a suspected tax evader could heve confessed his guilt, paid the evaded amount,
and absolved himself from further punishment".

25. Local Government Revenue Sources: In Japan se statistics, both the
prefectures and the cities, towns and villages are freguently referred to as
"Local governments". If a distinction-is made, the term "prefeotures and other
local governments" is apt to be used. The tax system, in itself, encouráges the
joint classification since the total tax rate is commonly divided into two parts
the prefectural rate and the city, town or village surtax rate. Generally
speaking, taxes may be divided into 3 classifications: Independent taxes, non-
legalized taxes and special purpose taxes. For example, on independent prefeo-
tural tax is one initially levied by the prefectures. Non-legaliz-d taxes are
those for which specific controlling provisions have not been established in the
law. Special object taxes are believed to indicate minor amounts, collected in
the form of taxes, for specific uses.

MEJORANDUM FOR: JAFANESE COVRNMENT
SUBJLCT:
Reorganization of National Tax Administration of the
Jarenese Government
1. The Fine-Point Economic Stabilization Directive of 19 December
1948 directed the Jaranese Government to edopt measures designed, among
other things, to "accelerate and strengthen the program of tax collection
and insure prompt, widespread and vigorou criminal prosecution of tax
evaders." These objectives, as well as the objective of fair and eguitable
tax administration, can be attained most effectively through the establish-
ment of a professional tax system staffed, insofar as possible, with per
sonnel of high moral and professional qualifications.
2. As a part of the program to accomplish these objectives, the
Japanese Government will at the earliest possible date take necessary
measures to:
a. Create within the inistry of Finance a Tax Administration
Bureau as an external bureau for the administration of national taxa-
tion. This bureau should have comrlete authority over its regional and
local offices, and be responsible for all national taxation matters except
those shown below under b.
b. Transfer to the created bureau all taxation functions of the
present Taxation Bureau, except those functions dealing with tax legislation
and research pertaining to tax legislation. The excepted functions should
be retained within a Taxation Research Bureau, an internal bureau of the Mini-
stry of Finance.
c. Divorce regulation of all matters alien to taxation from the regional
financial bureaus and reconstitute these bureaus as regional tax offices and
integral parts of the external Tax Administration Bureau.
d. Transfer all functions alien to taxation of the regional
financial bureaus to such agencies as the Minister of Finance may designate
in accordance with the reguirements for regional and district administration and
following the policy of simplification and consolidation of governmental activi-
ties.
e. Retain the Customs Division, now in the Taxation Bureau, within
the internal organization of the Ministry of Finance.
f. Provide tax personnel who are competent, qualified and trained,
insofar as possible, and adequate in number to administer and enforce vigorously
but fairly, the tax laws of the Government.
3. Direct communication between the Economic and Scientific Section,
General Headquarters, Supreme Comnander for the Allied Powers, and the Ministry
of Finance regarding matters within the sccpe of this memorandum is authorized.
FOR THE SUFR-ME COLMANDER:
R. M. LEVY,
Colonel,AGD,
Adjutant General

SUBJCT: Japanese Monopoly Bureau.
1 Organization.
a. The Japanese Monopoly is a Bureau of the Ministry of Pinance
respnsible for the crccuction, manufacture and distribution of tobacco, sa
and camphor products. For administration of these duties the monopoly is bro
down as follows:
Head Office, Tolkyo
16 Local onopoly or Regional Offices. These offices have supervi-
sion over two or more provinces.
600 Branch offices. These offices are directly responsible for sale
distributicn and in some instances the manufacture of. tobacco products and salt.
The offices ti:at are located in tobacco growing districts are also responsiblo
for the rurchase of leaf tobacco.
Total number of employees of the monopoly is 40,000. This includes
administration, sale and manufacturing.
b. As of 1 June 1949 the Monopoly Bureau will change over to a public
corporation under control of the Ministry of Finance. The new Monopoly Public
Corporation will be a juridical person of public law but, it will not be
considered a commercial company vithin the meaning of articles of the Japanese
Civil Code. The main reason for changing the Monopoly Bureau to a cublic
corporation is to change the status of personnel so they will not be coneidered as
public servants.
2. Tobacco Section.
Of thc three commodities controlled-by the Monopoly Bureau, tobacco
is the most important as a source of revenue. The laonopoly Bureau is the manu-
facturer, distributor and wholcsaler of all tobacco products.
The Monopoly Bureau issues license to the farmer to grow tobacco and whon
the tobacco is harvested the farmer is required to sell all of his tobacco to
the Monopoly Bureau. The Monopoly maintains and operates experimental farme 
growing leaf tobacco. Improved methods of cultivation, use of fertilize
curing are passed on to the farmer free of charge.
There, are cight re-drying factories for re-dryin

TO
DATB:7/21/49
SUBJECT:
Encloses and discusses memorandum of June 8, 1949 by Henry
Shavell, Deputy Chief, Internal Revenue Division, ESS, GHQ, on
the subject "Japanese Taxation of Commercial Entrants". Also
enclosed is a cony of a statement on the taxation problem pre-
pared by representatives of Standard-Vacuum, Yokohama, for Dr.
Carl S. Shoup, head of Army tax mission.
Basic question: What should be done to minimize the re-
pressive effects of the Japanese tax structure upon foreign
capital investment?
5
For a definition of SCAP policy the memorandum is intended
1
as a guide to consideration of the problems involved. It was
2
discussed with inconclusive results in a meeting of June 17 at
ESS, attended by two USPOLAD representatives. The attempts of
9
Dr. Fine of Ess to direct attention to the broad aspects of the
problem were frustrated by certain SCAP officials at the meetingN
who were determined to reach a decision on the immediate problemo
discussed below, of taxation of imported cigarettes and liquors.4
This is the problem in broad outline. Income in dollars and
other von-yen currency is temporarily exempt from taxation by
virtue of SCAPIN 4938-A. If existing tax rates were suddenly ap-
plied it would be a serious blow to foreign investors. Even now
a serious texation problem exists for yen income of commercial
entrante. Foreign corporations pay the national government tax
of 35% plus excess profits and local government levies bringing
C
the effective taxation rate on their net income to 60%. Excess
profits taxes begin where annual earnings exceed 30% of capital-
ization but because Japanese corporations are for the most part
greatly under-capitalized about three-fourths of them paid excess
profits taxes last year.
Personal income taxes would also be prohibitively high with-
out present restrictions. A $10,O00 salary would be subject to
a $7,500 tax. Even under SCAPIN 4938-A investment incomes of
π
commercial entrants are taxable. A return of $5,000 in a $50,000
ε
investment, for instance, 1s reduced to $1,666 by taxation.
did
Non-occupation cigarettes and liquors are now entering Japan
duty-free. Eight or nine companies, like Williams International,
have been authorized to sell imported and indigenous goods to
cat---
forms

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
forms of foreign exchange. These are destined for Japanese con-
sumption at yen equivalents ranging from $4 to $5 per carton.
10,O00 cases have been or ere being shipped into Japan. As com-
petition for the tobacco monopoly these cigarettes will represent
a tax loss to the government of 500 million yen and the 375,000
profit derived by the companies will likewise be tax-exempt.
SCAP has reportedly never inetructed the Japanese to exempt
non-occupation commodities from duties and taxes. When questioned
on the point, Japanese officials claim they cannot enforce appli-
cable duties beceuse they do not yet control al! major ports and
airfields. A more plausible exnlanation is that they feel that
severe criticism would result if existing tariffs and taxes were
improved. The CIF Yokohama price of a 1949 Chevrolet would come
to $6,800. Imoorted clothing would increase in price from 80% to
100%. Cigarettes would be sublect to 355% ad valorem customs
duty. The Japanese are most reluctant to revise their tariffs
downward wishing to wait until after the treaty when such revisions
may be used in bargaining for trade agreements with other nations.
Nevertheless, the government has'lately been pressing for "advice
from SCAp" on the problem.
The time hee come to reviee the nortion of SCAPIN 4938-A
XA
which prohibits taxation of non-yen income. The intent of this
provision was to protect commercial entrants whose earnings were
not derived from within the Japanese economy. Its effect was to
encourage the entry of licencees like the Coca Cola Company and
various banks which served the needs of the occupation forces.
On the other hand, operation in the yen economy of the Readers
Digest and kindred enternrisee were taxable. This anproach was
considered less likely to raise questions of extrality than a
distinction based upon non-Jananese nationals onerating within
the Japanese economy as distinguished from those operating with-
in the occupation forces economy. However, the scope of activity
permitted to commercial entrants has greatly increased and with
this the dividing line between internal and occupation economies
has dimmed. Those many foreign comnanies dealing in both yen
end allied currencies have kept separate books to take advantage
of the exemptions. This leads to obvious comolicatione including
temptatione in questions of accounting judgment. Certain Japanese
firms also onerate within this twilight zone and claim exemption
in their won-yen dealings. The more commercial restrictions are
liberalized the more unsatisfactory the exemption system becomes.
As regards the overall problem, three courses of action are
open to SCAP. First, it can maintain that the Jananese should
voluntarily remove alleged tax imnediments to the foreign invest-
ments program. This ie no solution as the Japanese are reluctant
to encourage a too rapid foreign investment program, fearing ex-
ploitation. Second, it can call for preferential tax rates for
foreign

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.98)
一3 -
foreign business. This is contrary to our own historio polioies
as well as to that of the FEC. Third, it can call for an inte-
gration of this problem with overall tax reform.
Substantial reductions in tax rates - primarily in Personal
Income Tax, secondarily in the Corporation Tax - may be possiblo
without significant overall loss of revenue. Further improvements
in tax administration will result in greatly increased receipts.
A cut-off in personel income tax, say at 50%, could be balanoed
by lowering the starting rate and increasing the spread of certain
brackets. As for the orporation tax, the excess profits component
is the chief complaint of foreign investors. This might be re-
pealed outright and the normal net income tax (now 35%) graduated
to compensate for the resulting loss in revenue. It is also sug-
gested that Article 6 of the tex law which provides a three-year
tax exemption for new corporations engaged in the mining and pro-
cessing of certain staple products might be extended to all new
concerns or for those founded by foreign investment.
As for tariffs, the guiding principle should be that they
be imposed on all non-occupation imports. Still, complete appli-
cation of tariffs and sales taxes might discourage investment by
foreignors. Therefore, until tariffs and taxes are revised to
more rational levele SCAP should attempt to protect those imports
calculated to assist the recovery effort. It would be unpolitio
for SCAP to recommend precisely what tariff changes should be
made. On the other hand, considering that duty-free and tax-free
sales of foreign cigarettes and liquors are reducing revenues
seriously, SCAP should not object to immediate application of
existing duties and taxes on these items.
The statement for Dr. Shoup by representatives of Socony-
Vacuum of Yokohama reviews the difficulties for foreign business
posed by the tax structure end makes recommendations for reform.
Personal income of foreign nationals should be taxed only to the
extent that it is actually drawn in Japan or remitted to Japan.
The application of the excess profits tax should be improved by
a more precise definition of the term "capital". One suggestion
is that capital be considered to be the excess of gross assets
over liabilities. In the interest of efficiency it is also re-
commended that the national government pre-empt the tax-collect-
ing functions of trral and prefectural governments.

ACTION

sir:
I have the honor to enclose a copy of a menorandum dated June 
1949, on the subject "Japanese Taxation of Conmercial Entrants," 
DCR
3
pared by Mr. Henry SHaVELL, Deputy Chief, Internal Revenue Divisi
FE
Economic and Scientific Section (Ess), General Headquarters, Sup
ITP
Commander for the Allied Powers. Copies of this memorandum were 
OLI+
culated by the Internal Revenue Division to interested sections a
COM
divisions of General Headguarters as enclosure to a check sheet
TREAS
dated June 9, 1949, with the reguest that coments be supplied
FRB.
8
them on various points. Additionally, the memorandwm was the
of a conference on June 17, 1949, participated in by representatis
of the concerned SCAP sections.
It is clearly stated that "the memorandum in no way represents
established SCAP policy, but is intended to serve chiefly as a pint
of departure for discussions..." The memorandun itself contains 
numbered paragraphs, the first of vhich is devoted to a brief into-
duction, with paragraphs 2 to 19 inclusive being devoted to the na-
ture of. the problem of Japanese taxation of comaercial entrants,
paragraphs 20 to 29 inclusive to possible courses of action, and
paragraphs 30 to 33 inclusive to a relatively brief sumary.
The nature of the problem is aptly described in the second
graph of the memorandwn, which concludes ith the statement that
basic question is this: what action by either SCAP or the Japanese
Government, or both, should be taken to minimize tho repressive ef-
fect upon foreign capital investment exercised by the existing Jap-
anese tax structure?"
In its check sheet of June 9, the ESs Internal Revemue Division
referred to "the importance and widespread ramifications" of the
probfen
RESTRICTED
ACTION COPY

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
problem and stated that it was desirable "to obtain the pertinent
views and all shades of opinions of all interested SCAP Sections
and Divisions." Specific comment on at least the following points
was requested: (a) other aspects, if any, of the problem not
covered in the memorandum (of June 8, 1949); (b) solutions to the
problem of conflict between Japanese tax application and SCAP pro
gram of foreign capital investment in Japan; (c) need for revi-
sion of SCAPIN 4938-A; (d) application of customs and internal
taxes on imports of commercial entrants; and (e) advisability of
and practicability of extending preferential tax treatment to com-
mercial entrants.
At the June 17 meeting, which was presided over by Dr. Sherwood
M. FINE, Director for Economics and Finance, ESS, and attended by
two representatives of this Mission, inconclusive discussions were
held on Mr. Shavell's memorandum. Initial attention was directed
to paragraphs 5 to 12 inclusive regarding application of Japanese
customs and consumption taxes to such goods imported into Japan
as are destined for non-Occupation consumption, especially cigarettes
and liquors. It was pointed out that this problem was immediate in
as much as a number of private firms had been licensed to import
and sell those items to non-Occupation purchasers having legal ac-
cess to acceptable foreign exchange. Sale of these items without
payment of duties or taxes assertedly would cause the Japanese
Government to lose considerable potential revenue from duty and tax
sources and would also compete directly with sale of Japanese cigar-
ettes by the Japanese Tobacco Monopoly and with tax-paid Japanese
liquors, thereby also reducing Japanese Government revenues.
The discussion brought out the desire of certain officials pre-
sent to make these imports of cigarettes and liquor subject to Jap-
anese duties and taxes in the expectation of permitting the Japanese
Government to realize about Yen 120 billion from cigarettes and Yen
65 billion from liquor during the 1949-50 fiscal year which commenced
April 1, 1949. The cigarettes would, it was stated, be subject to
the regular import duty of 355 per cent ad valorem, while liquors
would be subject to the regular excise taxes (see this Mission's des-
patch no. 330 dated May 23, 1949, "Transmittal of Memorandum entitled
'General Survey of Japanese Taxation'.")
Efforts of Dr. Fine and others at the meeting to focus attention
on the broader aspects of Japanese taxation on foreign businessmem,
especially as related to SCAP's program for encouragement of foreign
capital investment in Japan, were countered by those SCAP officials
intent upon reaching a decision, even though of a tentative nature,
regarding the immediate problem of taxation of cigarettes and liquors.
During the course of the discussion, mention was made of the desira-
bility of permitting the American and British Chambers of Commerce,
Tokyo, to study and comment upon Mr. Shavell's memorandum. Mr. Harold
L. MOSS, Chief, Internal Revenue Division, ESS, stated that the memo-
randum could not be declassified owing to mention therein of certain
American and other firms, and that the Chambers in question had al-
ready been invited to present their views on the Japanese taxation
problem to the Internal Revenue Division for reference to Dr. Carl S.
SHOUP, head of the special Department of the Army taxation mission
now in Japan.
Among
RESTRICTED

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
Toiyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
-3-
Among those who spoke of the broader aspects of Japanese taxa-
tion of commercial entrants, including corporate and income taxes,
was Mr. J. E. PENNYBACKER, official in the Petroleum Advisory Group,
G-4 Section, General Headquarters. Mr. Pennybacker, former employee
of a prominent American oil company, stated that several American
firms were having difficulty in recruiting young men to come to Japan
owing to the high cost of living and imminent threat of becoming sub-
ject to Japanese income taxes. He added that, should the Japarese
authorities be permitted to collect corporation and income taxes at
the rates presently fixed, foreign business men would be forced to
2/
leave Japan. Enclosed is a copy of an undated memorandum entitled
"Proposals for Tax Legislation," from which Mr. Pennybacker read ex-
tracts at the meeting. These proposals are generally similar to
those contained in Mr. Shavell's memorandum. They are of interest,
however, as a presentation of the views of certain legal experts of
a prominent American concern vitally interested in the problem of
Japanese taxation of foreign business men and concerns.
The inconclusive June 17 meeting closed with the understanding
that interested SCAP sections and divisions would submit their com-
ments on Mr. Shavell's memorandum as speedily as possible, upon re-
ceipt of which another meeting would be called to go into the matter
further.
614.7
The Department will recall that the question of excessively high
taxation in Japan has been raised on several occasions in recent
months by the American Chamber of Commerce in Japan.  Reference in
this connection is made to this Mission's despatch no) 192 dated
farch 31, 1949, transmitting a copy of a memorandum prepared by the
Chamber summarizing deterrents to American postwar investment in
Japan. The problem of taxation was taken up in this memorandum in
rather general terms; however, comparison of the Chamber's comments
with Mr. Shavell's memorandum indicates that some of these conments
are covered in the memorandum.
While no definite policy has yet been adopted in General Head-
quarters regarding courses of action to be taken with respect to
Japanese taxation of commercial entrants, it may be noted that three
possible courses are mentioned in Mr. Shavell's memorandum. The
first involves negative action; for example, General Headquarters
can maintain that the Japanese Government should voluntarily take
action to remove alleged tax impediments to its foreign investment
program. A second course would be to recommend to the Japanese
authorities that preferential tax rates be applied to the incomes
of foreign investors for a given period. A third course might in-
volve careful consideration of this problem for integration with
overall tax reform plans.
The first suggested course is not favored for reasons stated
in paragraph 2l of Mr. Shavell's memorandum. The second course,
involving preferential tax rates, is likewise not favored owing to
the issue of extraterritoriality, and creation of "islands of tax
exemption" (quoting a phrase used by Dr. Fine at the June 17 meeting),
which would be considered discriminatory by the Japanese authorities
ardundoubtedly attacked by them soon after the peace treat between
Japen and the Allied Powers was signed. The third course of action
is
RESTRICTED

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
Töky's Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
is considered to be the most desirable since it would obviate the
need for preferential tax treatment for foreign investors and, to
a certain extent, might also lessen the need for the non-yen our-
rency exemption provided for in SCAPIN 4938-A of November 29, 1947,
under which non-Japanese nationals are now exampt from Japanese taxs-
tion. Paragraphs 24 to 29 of the memorandum contain suggestions for
possible downward revision of Japanese corporate and income taxss,
and also import duties and excise taxes.
The positior. with regard to the recommended future course of
action is summarized as follows:
"The existing Japanese tax structure, notably
the Personal Income Tax and the Corporation Tax,
militates against the SCAP and Japanese Government
program to attract foreign capital investment to
Japan. It would appear unwise for SCAP to press
for special preferential tax treatment for foreign
investors as such action might result in adverse
criticiam by other nations. The most practical and
expedient solution to this problem would appear to
lie in the over-all reduction of Japanese personal
income tax progression and in the elimination or
revision of the excess profits tax component of the
Corporation Tax. There would seem to be a possibi-
lity of substantial reduction in income tax rates
without materially reducing income tax revenues."
In the opinion of this Mission, Mr. Shavell's memorandum is a
competent presentation of the problem of Japanese taxation of com-
mercial entrants as related to the question of attraction of foreign
investment to Japan. Adoption of the recommendations made therein
seemingly would do much to alleviate the fears of foreign business
men over the imminent threat of being subject to the excessively high
Japanese taxes, and would undoubtedly also serve to improve Japan's
status as a potential field for foreign investment.
Respectfully yours,
10
Cloyce K. Huston
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim

MIMORANDUM FOR FILE:

SUBJECT: Japaneme Taxation of Commercial Entrants.
1. The purpose of this memorandum is to outline the nature of
the problem involving application of Japanese taxes to commereial
entrants and thoir firms and to discuss possible courses of action
by scap pertinent to this problem. The memorandum in no way repre-
sents established SCAP policy, but is intended to serve chiefly as
a point of departure for discussions with other interested ss
divisions and SCAP sections.
NATURE OF THE PROBLEM:
2. One of the more important SCAP programs of the moment calls
for the attraction of foreign capital investment to Japan for the
purpose of accelerating Japanese economic rehabilitation and mini-
mising the cost of United States economic assistance for Japan. In
addition to this, SCaP is also endeavoring to turn more and more of
ordinary government functions back to the Japanese as well as to
bring about a gradual restoration of normal trade and commereial re-
lations between Japan and the rest of the world. The application of
existing Japanese taxes, particularly personal income and corporation
taxes to foreigmers in Japan would, if applied completely, militate
agaiast mmch ScAP objectives. While income in dollars or other non-
yem ourrency now earned in Japan is exempt from Japansse taxes by
virture of SCaPIN 4938-A, this exemption is only temporary and will
ccatinme in force until such time as the SCAPIN is rescinded or a
Japenese peace treaty is conoluded. An immediate problem arises,
however, with respect to the yen income derived by certain commereial
entrants who have been, or are in the process of being, authorised te
make fareign investments in Japan. The basic question is this: What
actiem by either SCAP or the Japanese Government, or both, should be
takem in order to minimize the repressive effect upon foreign eapital
imvestment exercised by the existing Japanese tax structure?
3. Gorporate Taxation and Commarcial Antrants: A foreign cor-
poratiem in Japan is mubject to the following major taxes: A natioz
gavermment carporation net inoome tax of 35%, an additional 10 to 2
emsess profits tax beginning at the point when annual earnings exoeed
30s af the capital account, and a local government net income tax of
las. Imasmch as the lax local government net income tax is dedmstiale

as a business expense in computing national government corporation
tax liability, the total effective rate on corporation net income is
actually 46.7% rather than the sum of 35% and 18%. 1/ The excess
profits tax, although limited to annual earnings that exceed 30% of
the capital stock and surplus reserve aggregate is actually applied
to the majority of corporations. This is due primarily to the fact
that Japanese corporations are for the most part greatly under-capi-
talized. For the fiscal year 1948-49, for example, no less than
86,033 of the 115,128 Japanese corporations reporting taxable in-
comes were subject to the excess profits tax. Excess profits tax
revenue for the same year accounted for alxost 1/3 of the entire
proceeds of the corporation tax. Thus, it would appear that the
average Japanese corporation during the 1948-49 fiscal year had been
subject to an effective corporation rate in the order of 60% (46.7%
net income and a conservative average of 13% to 15% of excess prc-
fits tax liability). The high effective rate of corporate taxation
coupled with the facts that Japanese depreciation rates are extremely
low in relation to replacement costs and that the concept of Japanese
taxable profit covers such questionable items as upward revaluation
of fixed assets, does appear to constitute adequate cause for hesi-
tancy by potential foreign investors.
4. Personal Income Taxation and Cormercial Entrants: The exis-
ting personal income tax rate structure, which may be excessive even
when applied to the Japanese internal economy, hecomes almost pro-
hibitively heavy when applied to medium income levels in the dollar
economy. For example, an American emplcyee of a foreign firm in Japan
whose annual salary is $10,000 woul? be subjected to an income tax
liability of roughly 75% under the existing Japanese income tax (S10,000
equivalent to 3,é million yen per annum). While such income in dsllars
is not presently taxable in Japan under SCAPIN 4938-A, this ineome
will becone taxable at the conclusion of the Japanese peace treaty, if
not before. Even at present, however, investment incomes of cenmercial
entrants resident in Japan are taxable y ths Japanese. For exampl.,
a foreign na i nsl in Japan may invest 50, C00 in establishing a Jap
anese plant. His investment would, at the present exchange rate be
valued at 18 million yen. If he were to earn, say, a l06 raturn on
his investment, he would receive l.& million yen annually before taxes.
While this annual income would be eqtivalent to only $5, o00, the exis-
ting Japanese income tax would reduce lis 1,8 million yen annual in-
come by approximately 66%..

customs and consumption taxes to such goods imported into Japan as
are destined for non-occupation consumption. At the present time
Japanese customs and internal taxes are in general not imposed upon
such imports. The CSS (Overseas Supply Store), under the manage-
ment of ESS-Trade Service: Division, presently imports a wide variety
of commodities for resale to all non-occupation purchasers having
legal access to acceptable foreign exchange. Thus, commercial en-
trants and other non Japanese nationals may purchase imported auto-
mobiles, cigarettes, liquor, clothing, cosmetics, appliances, etc.,
from OSs, duty and tax free, so long as they can legally possess
and acquire foreign exchange. Moreover, commercial entrants in
Japan may also place individual import orders by filing application
with the EsS-Foreign Trade Division. It is understood that approxi-
mately 4,000 of such individual licenses to import have been val1-
dated by ESS-Foreign Trade. Commercial entrants may thereby import
supplementary supplies (presumably or allegedly for personal use)
duty and tax free.
6. Technically, however, no legal basis exists for the exemp-
tion of customs and interral taxes for such imports. This Head-
quarters has never formally (or, to the writer's knowledge, infor-
mally) instructed the Japanese Government to exempt such iuports
from existing customs and consumption taxes. Except for an insig-
nificant amount of customs duties collected from petroleum product
imports, customs and consumption taxes legally applicable to non-
occupation imports have not been onforced. Japanese taxation and
customs officials have heretofore chosen to ignore the law as it
would apply to such imports, this apparently with the sanction of
the Japanese Government. Mien queried on this point, Japanese
customs officials indicated that they have not been in a position
to enforce applicable duties because they have not been as yet
given control of all major ports. These officials also state that
they have not been in a position to check incoming air cargo.
Notwithstanding these excuses, however, it is probable that the
Japanese Government has consciously refused to enforce their cus-
toms and tax laws with respect to non-occupation imports for other
reasons.
7. The full application of existing customs and internal taxes
on non-occupation imports would result in extremely high taxes on
many commodity lines. The application of a lo0% ad valorem com-
modity tax on top of a 70% ad valorem customs duty on standard size
passenger cars imported into Japan would raise the CIF Yokohama
value of a new 1949 Chevrolet to about $6,800. Items of clothing
would in general be increased by from 80% to 100% with the appli-
cation of existing tariffs and internal taxes. Liquor items would
be increased by from 150% to 300%. Cigarettes would be subject t.o
a 355% ad valorem rate under the customs duty. Certain construction
materials would be raised by roughly l00%.
8. Because the application of such levels of custom duties
and internal taxes would probably create unfavorable criticism of
the Customs Law, Commodity Tax Law, and other tax laws application
to imports, the Japanese have heretofore preferred to ignore en-
forcement of these laws. The Japanese authorities are extremely
reluctant

reluctant to revise dow ward their Customs Law. While they can
see the need for wholesale reductions in tariff rates, they qiite
naturally would like to defer revisions in their tariff laws until
the post-treaty period when they will be in a more advantageous
position to utilize tariff cuts as bargaining aids in obtaining
trade agreements with other nations. Both the Finance and Inter-
nal Revenue Divisions of ESS have repeatedly pointed out to the
Japanese officials the advantages of immediate downward revision
of tariffs but to no avail. Japan requires little in the way of
a protective tariff. From a revenue standpoint, Japan can afford
to drastically slash tariff rates and not significantly affect
tariff revenue potentials.
9. The problem of application of customs duties and consump-
tion taxes to non-occupation imports has now been brought to a head.
Some eight or nine commercial entrants have recently been authorized
to sell imported (as well as indigenous) goods to all commercial en-
trants and others having access to dollars, sterling, and other as-
ceptable forms of foreign exchange. Thus, these firms will be in
direct competition, so to speak, with the Overseas Supply Store.
Information now reaching this office reveals that the initial im-
ports of these new concorns are largely made up of cigarettes and
liquor. One company alone, the Williams International, has imported
from 4, 000 to 5,000 cases of American cigarettes. It is probable
that some 10, 00o cases of cigarettes have been, or are in the process
of being, shipped to Japan for resale by these new concerns. In view
of the extremely small and limited market that can purchase from OSS
and the new firms (there are only 6,O00 total OSS cards outstanding,
of which an estimated 1,200 represent commercial entrants and their
employees and the remainder composed of non-Japanese nationals -
missionaries, foreign missions, Chinese, etc.) it is readily apparent
that large quantities of these cigarettes are destined for consump-
tion by Japanese. For example, because American cigarettes have a
high black market value among the Japanese (yen equivalent ranging
from $4 to $5 per carton), American cigarettes are rationed to both
Occupation personnel (in PX's) and commercial entrants (in OSS).
At the PX ration allowance of one carton per week for every adult,
it would take approximately two years for the new concerns to dis-
pose of their present cigarette orders - assuming also that Oss dis-
continued their cigarette sales completely. It is quite probable
that all 10,000 cases of cigarettes could be disposed of within a
relatively short-time if quantity ration restrictions were not i-
posed. Cigarettes, in the absence of customs duties, are highly
profitable sales items. The OSS retails cigarettes at $1.50 per
carton, thereby realizing a profit of roughly 75 cents on each
carton.
10. The resale or barter of American cigarettes in the Jap-
anese market costs the Japanese Government untold millions, if not
billions, of yen in revenue. The Japanese Government tobacco mono-
poly constitutes the second most important single source of revenue

they desire to impose applicable custons and internal taxes on OSS
imports and all other non-occupation imports. We have replied that

13. A problem also arese concerning taxability of the so-
called "invisible exports". Until last January, OSS purchases of
Japanese produced merchandise for resale for dollars was exempt
from applicable Japanese taxes. The argument advanced for tax exemp-
tion was that sales of Japanese goods for dollars constituted a form
of invisible exports and that such invisible exports were to be given
the same tax exempt treatment as was applicable to ordinary exports.
The Japanese Government, however, argued that indigenous goods con-
sumed in Japan by non-occupation users, whether sold for yen or
foreign exchange, were not to be tax exempt as they were not physi-
cally exported. To exempt such goods from taxation would discriminate
against Japanese merchants who sell the same commodities inclusive
of tax. Accordingly, ESs adopted the policy in January of this year
of offering no objection to Japanese Government efforts to collect
internal taxes on such indigenous goods as are sold through Oss.

14. SCAPIN 4938-A. The only authorized tax exemption opera-
ting in favor of commercial entrants in Japan at the present time
is provided for in SCAPIN 4938-A, a copy of which is attached hereto.
Under this SCAPIN, the Japanese Government was prohibited from:
a. Imposing taxes that discriminate against non-Japanese
nationale;
b. Imposing taxes upon United Nation Nationals and thei:
property which taxes were primarily designed to meet charges falling
upon Japan as result of the war; and
c. Imposing taxes measured by or based upon income law-
fully received on non-yen currency.
15. Parts a and b of the above paragraph represents policy
decision of the Far Eastern Commission in Washington. Part c of
the above paragraph, however, was initiated by this Headquarters as
an interim measure designed to prevent the Japanese Government from
taxing the earnings of commercial entrants when such earnings were
not derived from operations within the Japanese domestic economy.
The principle upon which the latter provision was based uponies as
follows; where income was derived from within the Japanese domss-
tic economy, whether by Japanese or by commercial entrants, such
income was to be taxable by the Japanese Government; where, how-
ever, income was derived by commercial entrants primarily from ser-
vices for the Occupation Forces or to other commercial entrants,
which income did not originate from within the Japanese domestic
economy, such income was not to be subject to Japanese taxation.
The reason for this provision was to encourage and facilitate the
entry into Japan of SCAP licensees who provided essential servics
to the Occupation Forces or who directly facilitated attainment f
SCAP objectives. Thus, the Coca Cola Company, the Pepsi Cola Com-
pany, various foreign banks, etc., which provided services for th
Occupation Forces, were not to be subject to Japanese income taxes
as the income of such firms was not derived from the Japanese eccucmy.
Contrariwise, incomes derived by Time Magazine, Newsweek Magazine,
The Central Motion Picture Exchange, and the Readers Digest, from
sales within the yen economy, were to be subject to Japanese taxation.
Under the then existing foreign exchange regulations and restrictions,
it was not possible for Japanese nationals to deal in currency other
than in yen. Because of this, in the drafting of the SCAPIN, the dis-
tinction between yen and non-yen incomes was utilized rather than a
distinction based upon non Japanese nationals operating within the
Japanese economy as distinguished from those operating within ti
pation Foroes economy. It was felt that the adoption of the former
exemption basis was preferable to the latter in that it would be less
likely to raise questions of extension of extra-territoriality righi:
16. Need for Revision of SCAPIN 4938-A: In view of the fact
that the scope of activities of commercial entrants in Japan has beer.
greatly extended since November 1947 (when SCAPIN 4938-A was issued),
there may now be a need for re-examination of the provisions of SCAPIN
4938-A. The dividing line between what constitutes the internal as dis-
tinguished from the occupation economy has grown less determinate with
every
RESTRICTED

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
Enolosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28,1949.
every successive liberalization of commercial entrant restrictions.
A sort of "twilight zone" has come into existence, encompassing both
Occupation and Japanese internal activities. Foreign banks, for ex-
ample now handle yen accounts and other forms of internal Japanese
banking operations. A number of commercial entrants have been recent-
ly authorized to retail both imported and indigenous merchandise to
other commercial entrants and to other individuals in Japan having
access to foreign exchange. The need for exempting the incomes from
such concerns from Japanese taxes is no longer as pressing as was
the case when SCAPIN 4938-A was issued. Such concerns as Barclay
and Company, Williams International, Dodwell and Company, and others,
are in competition with Japanese firms who are taxable, except for
the fact that the former sell for dollar instruments while the lat-
ter sell in yen currency. This is clearly a case of extension of
extra-territoriality rights by SCAP.
17. In order to avail themselves of the tax exemption provided
for in SCAPIN 4938-A, these commercial entrants dealing in coth yen
and non-yen areas have been maintaining two sets of accounts, one
for yen, and the other for non-yen, dealings. Under this technique,
certain aggregate dollar costs which are applicable to yen and non-
yen operations, have been permitted to be apportioned and translated
into yen costs at various sets of exchange rates (determined by tax-
payers with the approval of the Japanese Government). Thus, the
dollar cost of story material and other supplies furnished to the
Readers Digest office in Japan has been converted to a yen basis in
order to arrive at constructive costs to be offset against yen gross
income. This has quite obviously led to many complications. This
office has suggested to the Japanese Taxation Bureau that they adopt
a more or less liberal attitude toward approving exchange conversion
rates desired by various commercial entrants in computing construc-
tive yen costs. As a result, the Japanese Government has offered
no objection to the use of varied exchange rates by Time Magazine,
Newsweek, Readers Digest, Central Motion Picture Exchange, New York
Times and various other SCAP licensees in determining constructive
yen costs. However, now that the range of licensee and commercial
entranttactivities has been greatly increased, complications resul-
ting from maintaining separate yen versus non-yen accounts have mul-
tiplied. A case in point is that of foreign banks now operating
within the yen economy. What portion of each bank's investments, in
both dollar and yen terms, and what portion of each bank's fixed costs
are to be deductible from yen. gross incomes and what portion from non-
yen gross incomes?
18. Also operating within this "twilight zone" are various
Japanese concerns. For example, the Japanese automobile companies.
which have been appointed distributors for General Motors, Chrysler,
Willys-Overland, and the Austin Company, have been selling for foreign
exchange. Conceivably, however, such concerns can state that their
income was "measured by or based upon" incomes in non-yen currencies,
and thereby. seek to obtain exemption from Japanese taxes. Foreign
insurance concerns operating in Japan (American International Under-
writers, Home. Insurance Company) have been largely off-setting their
yen labor and operating costs by taking advantage of SCAPIN 4938-A.
An interesting example of how this is done was furnished last month.

Enclosure No. 1 to

The Home Insurance Company (an American firm) had insured an Ameri-
can officer's new 1949 Ford for $2185. The car was wrecked, and the
officer received full value from the Home Insurance Company.  The
insurance company in turn sold the wrecked remains of the 1949 Ford
to the Governor of Kanagawa Prefecture for 1,600,000 yen (or the
equivalent of $4,400)!
19. SCAPIN 4938-A has operated to extend preferential tax
treatment to a number of non-Japanese nationals and stateless in-
dividuals who have been residents of Japan since pre-war days. For
example, a Korean national who has resided in Japan for the past
dozen or more years is employed by the Foreign Broadcast Information
Service (a U.S. Government agency with a branch in Tokyo) and is paid
a dollar salary by a U.S. Government agency in Japan. This Korean
now claims exemption from the Japanese income tax, and from that part
of the citizens tax which is based upon income. Numerous cases are
known to exist where Chinese and other non-Japanese nationals have
acquired dollar and other non-yen earnings by representing foreign
concerns in Japan. SCAPIN 4938-A permits such individuals, who have
been continuously resident in Japan since pre-war days, who have
hitherto been fully taxable by the Japanese Government, and who share
a responsibility in supporting the Japanese Government along with
other Japanese residents, to claim tax exemption under a SCAPIN which.
was never intended to cover such individuals. In view of the above
(para. 16-18), and because of the further complications that will in-
evitably arise from anticipated further liberalization of commercial
entrant restrictions, that part of SCAPIN 4938-A that pertains to pro-
hibition of Japanese taxes on non-yen income should be re-examined
with a view to revision.
B. COURSES OF ACTION:
20. With respect to the problem of repressive effects of Jap-
anese taxation on foreign capital investments, there appear to be
three major courses of action open to SCAP. The first course involves
negative action. This Headquarters can maintain, for example, that the
Japanese Government should voluntarily take action to remove alleged
tax impediments to its foreign investment program. A second course
would be to recommend to the Japanese that preferential tax rate be
applied to the incomes of foreign investors for a given period. A
third course might involve careful consideration of this problem for
integration with overall tax reform plans.
21. The negative course of action outlined in the above para-
graph constitutes no solution. It is not at all certain that Japanese
policy-makers regard the need for foreign capital investment in Japar
in the same light as that existing in this Headquarters. The Japanese
have exhibited a minor but nevertheless detectable degree of reluctance
on the question of taking positive action in order to stimulate foreign
capital investment in Japan. Perhaps this hesitancy stems from an ap-
prehension on their part that too rapid and too great a foreign in-
vestment
RESTRICTED

=== Page 188 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
-9-
vestment program at the present time might lead to possible exploi-
tation of Japanese interests by foreign elements. It. would appear
that it would be up to this Headquarters to adopt a more positive
solution to the foreign investment problem if the problem is to be
resolved at all.
22. The second course of action, that involving the application
of preferential tax rates to foreign investment incomes involves some
undesirable features from a political and diplomatic viewpoint. It
has been the aim of this Headquarters to steer clear of the thorny
issue of extra-territoriality rights. The U.S. Government has always
avoided seeking preferential tax treatment for its nationals in other
countries.4/SCAPINS issued by this Headquarters have been so worded
as to minimize the possibility of post-treaty challenges to Occupation
policies on the grounds that the Occupation authorities obtained
special benefits for their nationals by taking undue advantage of
their position. To place SCAP in the position of specifying that pre-
ferential tax treatment will be accorded to foreign nationals during
some part or the whole of the Occupation would appear to be unwise
politically and diplomatically. Moreover, this action if taken would
directly contravene Far Eastern Commission policy calling for the
application of Japanese taxes to non-Japanese nationals on a non-
discriminatory basis. Non-discrimination usually implies non-discrimi-
nation both for and against. To call for preferential treatment for
non-Japanese nationals would be to call for taxes that discriminate
against Japanese taxpayers.
23. The third course of action outlined under paragraph 20
above appears to be the most desirable of the three. This line of
action would obviate the need for preferential tax treatment for
foreign investors and, to a certain extent, might also lessen the
need for the non-yen currency exemption provided for in SCAPIN 4938-A.
Obviously, however, the degree of statutory reduction in Japanese taxes
would have to be principally conditioned by the amount of overall re-
venue loss that would result to the Japanese Government from such action.
24. It would appear that a substantial reduction in certain
Japanese tax rates (primarily in the Personal Income Tax and to a les-
ser extent in the Corporation Tax) could be met without, at the same
time, resulting in a significant decrease in realized tax revenue.
Despite the very substantial improvements in tax administration that
have taken place within the past two years, the ratio of actual tax
collections compared to theoretical levels of tax collection (based
upon perfect tax administration and tax compliance), is still inor-
dinately low. For the 1948-49 fiscal year less than 40% of the tax re-
venue that was potentially collectable from the non-farm self-assessed
group
Radio WCL 87752, dated 12 May 1946, from Joint Chiefs of Staff in
Washington to CINCAFPAC (MacArthur) reads in part "that it has
never been United States Government Policy to press for special
treatment of U.S. Nationals except where foreign laws have been
discriminatory."

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28,.1949.
-10 -
group was actually collected. It is quite possible that almost the
same amount of tax revenue that was collected could have been col-
lected under statutory rates of exemption considerably more liberal
than currently exist. It is still a common-place in Japan that in-
come taxes are assessed "backwards". The tax assessment is very often
arrived at by arbitration between taxpayer and tax collector, with very
little if any reference to statutory provision. Similarly, tax assess-
ments have been more recently influenced by external pressures, such
as assigned collection goals.
25. A cut-off in personal income tax progression, perhaps at the
50% level, might, in the final analysis, result in practically the same
realized income tax revenue. Such a reduction would at the same time
go far in resolving the problem of application of Japanese personal in-
come taxes to non-yen incomes of foreign nationals. A lowering of the
starting rate of the income tax, coupled with an increased spread in
bracket areas, would also work in the same direction but would pro-
bably involve more appreciable revenue losses.
26. With respect to the Corporation Tax, the excess profits com-
ponent lies at the root of much of the complaints by foreign investors.
Outright elimination of the excess profits tax would appear advantageous
from the standpoint of taxpayer morale and tax administration. Because
the excess profits tax is based upon corporate capital accounts which
for the most part are grossly understated in relation to earning power,
this tax discriminates unduly against corporations which have been in
existence prior to the war's end but which were not given the benefit
of re-capitalization at higher levels following imposition of the War
Indemnity Special Tax. 5/ Repeal of the excess profits tax might
largely obviate the need for permitting corporation re-valuation of
assets so as to allow more rational operation of the same tax. Repeal
of the excess profits tax, however, would initially involve some re-
venue decline. It might be possible, however, to graduate that normal
net income tax for corporations (which is at present a flat 35%) in
order to offset the whole or part of the revenue loss that would result
from excess profits tax repeal. Thus, it would be possible to reduce
foreign investor criticism of the existing corporation tax structure
(through the substitution of a graduated normal tax for an excess pro-
fits tax) and at the same time maintain the same degree of corporation
tax productivity.
27. Another
As a result of the War Indemnity Special Measures Tax, many of
Japan's largest corporations were made technically insolvent. Suc:
corporations were permitted to write-off a portion of their losses
through upward revaluation of assets. The revaluation of corporation
.assets permitted under the Enterprise Reconstruction and Reorgani-
zation Law and Under the Financial Institution Reorganisation and
Reconstruction Law were exempt from the corporation tax which would
normally have been imposed upon revaluation "profits". As a result,
certain Japanese corporations have been placed in a more advantageoud
position with respect to competitor concerns simply because competitor
concerns remained solvent following imposition of the War Indemnity Tax.

-11-
27. Another possibility in corporation tax revision would be
to expand the application of Article 6 of the Japanese Corporation
Tax Law. Article 6 present iy provides that:
"such corporations which are engaged in the
manufacturing, mining or picking of staple
products as may be designated by ordinance
may be, as prescribed by ordinance, exempt
from corporation tax in respect of the am-
ount of normal income accrued from certain
businesses during the accounting period in
which the corporations have begun said busi-
nesses or accounting periods ending within
three years from the first day of the fol-
lowing accounting period".
The Corporation Tax Law enforcement regulations specify (Article 2)
that the staple products referred tc in Article 6 of the Corporation
Tax Law are:
"Gold metals, lime-nitrogen, ammonium sulphate,
burnt potassium alum powder, burnt potassium
quartz trachyte powder and super phosphate
lime (including metamorphosis manure), gold
minerals and placer minerals, petroleum, coal
and peat, potassium quartz trachyte and phos-
phate cre."
It might be possible for Article 6 of the Corporation Tax Law to be
expanded to include exemption from tax for the first two or so years
of operation for all new concerns or for such concerns that came into
existence as a result of foreign capital investment.
28. SCAPIN 4938-A as it presently stands may provide too broad
a basis for exemption of non-yen income. Some provision for income
tax exemption for commercial entrants is highly desirable at present,
however, in view of the relatively high tax rates prevailing, the
distorted relationship between dollar and yen incomes, and the need
for attraction of foreign traders and foreign commercial entrants in
Japan. One expedient solution may be tc amend SCAPIN 4938-A so as to
limit Japanese tax exemption only to those individuals entering Japan
since September 2, 1945. This would permit the Japanese Government
to impose taxes on non-yen income earned by individuals continuously
resident in Japan since the end of the war but would prohibit non-
yen income taxation on those entering Japan since V-J Day.
29. With respect to the problem of customs and consumption tax
application to non-occupation imports, it is felt that:
a. As a principle, Japanese tariffs and consumption taxes
should be applied to all imports not destined for Occupation force
consumption;
b. Complete
RESTRICTED

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
Emolosire No. 1 to
Tokyo!s Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
-12-
b. Complete application of existing tariffs and consump-
tion taxes might be detrimental to SCAP programs calling for attrac-
tion of foreign capital investment to Japan and of forein buyers;
c. Until such time as the Japanese Government reduces
existing ad valorem tariff rates and excise taxes to more rational
levels, it is in the interests of SCAP to restrain the Japanese
Government from imposing tariffs and taxes on at least those cate-
gories of imports that would aid in the overall recovery effort of
Japan and which imports are not adequately available in the domestic
economy (e.g. - passenger automobiles, construction equipment, in-
dustrial machinery, etc,);
d. SCAP should, however, not be placed in the position of
recommending to precisely what levels existing tariffs should be
reduced;
e. In view of the facts that the continuation of duty and
tax free imports of tobacco (and to a lesser extent, liquor) are re-
ducing Japanese Government internal revenues, that tobacco and Liquor
are inadequate supply in the Japanese market, and that such goods do
not positively aid in Japanese reconstruction, SCAP should not offer
any objection to the immediate application of existing liquor and
tobacco custom duties and excise taxes.
f. SCAP should take a more active interest in having the
Japanese Government draft and submit legislation to the next Diet
session amending the Customs Law of Japan.
C. SUMMARY:
30. The existing Japanese tax structure, notably the Personal
Income Tax and the Corporation Tax, militates against the SCAP and
Japanese Government program to attract foreign capital investment to
Japan. It would appear unwise for SCAP to press for special preferen-
tial tax treatment for foreign investors as such action might result
in adverse criticism by other nations. The most practical and expe-
dient solution to this problem would appear to lie in the overall re-
duction of Japanese personal income tax progression and in the elimi-
nation or revision of the excess profits tax component of the Corpora-
tion Tax. There would seem to be a possibility of substantial reducti
in income tax rates without materially reducing income tax revenues.
31. While Japanese customs and internal taxes are currently not
imposed upon non-occupation imports, this practice is technically il-
legal. At the same time it would appear that existing tariffs and
excise taxes are so high that, if applied fully to all non-occupation
imports, they would be detrimental to SCAP foreign investment and
foreign trade programs.
32. The importation of American cigarettes and liquor for non-
-occupation consumption on a duty and excise tax-free basis offers
unfair

-13
unfair competition with indigenous products sdnce the lattes are
sales of indigenous tobacco and liquor product resulting from
"leakage" of American products into the Japanese market.
33. It would be desirable for the Shoup Taxation Misslon to
fully consider these problems when formulating recommendations on
Japanese tax reform. In this connection, it would be desirable
for all interested ESS Divisions and SCAP Sections to comment upon
the above discussion so that Professor Shoup may be advised of the
general consensus of opinion of GHQ.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
AG 012.2 (29 Nov 47)ESS/FI
APO 500
(SCAPIN 4938-A)
29 November 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR: JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
THROUGH:
Central Liaison Office, Tokyo
SUBJECT:
Applicability of Taxes to Non-Japanese Nationals
1. SCAPIN 1826-A, dated 25 July 1946, subject: "Applicability
of Ordinary Taxes to Non-Japanese Nationals" is hereby rescinded.
2. There is no objection to the applicability of local and na-
tional taxation to non-Japanese nationals except as specified in
paragraph 3 below, provided that such taxes are not discriminatory
against non-Japanese nationals. No tax, primarily designed to meet
reparations or other charges falling upon the Japanese Government as
a result of the war shall be imposed by the Japanese Government upon
United Nations nationals or their properties.
3. No tax will be imposed by the Japanese Govermment on the of-
ficial salaries of military personnel, of civilians attached to the
occupation forces, and of accredited personnel of foreign diplomatic
missions, delegations, and other official representation attached to
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers. No tax based upon or
measured by income lawfully received in non-yen currencies will be
imposed by the Japanese Government. This shall not however be con-
strued to prohibit the taxation of SCAP employees or SCAP licensee
employees paid in yen currencies.
4. Direct communication between the Ministry of Finance and in-
terested staff sections of this Headquarters is authorized to imple-
ment the provisions of this memorandum.
FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDER:
R. M. LEVY
Colonel, AGD
Adjutant General

Tokyo, on the subject wJapanese Taxe-

(Copy of statement prepared by representatives
of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, Yokohama,
for Dr. Carl S. Shoup, head of special Depart-
ment of the Army Tax Mission now in Japan)
PROPOSALS FOR TAX LEGISLATION
There are several tax matters which require new legislation if
foreign corporations are to survive in Japan. A remedy for each of
these matters is not only desirable but absolutely essential as
present tax laws make it virtually impossible for foreign corpora-
tions to conduct business in Japan with any hope of profit.
Though there are many tax difficulties in the present Japanese
tax system, the major points are briefly stated below:
I. TAXES UPON PERSONAL INCOME:
The present tax rates on personal income, as applied to the
ordinary American business salaries, approach confiscatory levels.
Salaries in excess of 1,000,000 per annum, though this amount is
less than $3,000, are taxed at the rate of 75%. Salaries of
¥5,000,000, the equivalent of less than $14,000, are taxed at the
maximum rate of 85%. It is entirely certain that it will be impos-
sible to persuade competent American executives to work in Japan if
their salaries are to be subjected to such rates.
It may be argued that the personal income tax rates in Japan
should be determined in accordance with Japanese income regulations,
standards and requirements, and that foreigners who elect to live in
Japan should be treated exactly like the Japanese. This point of view,
however, does not bear analysis since Japanese and foreigners working
in Japan live upon entirely different scales, have entirely different
expenses for their living requirements and have entirely different
objectives. Part of the foreigner's income will be spent in Japan for
his living expenses and his recreation but part of it will be spent,
ordinarily, in the United States for the care of his dependents who
are in the United States for educational or other reasons; part of it
will be spent for life insurance and other dollar charges which are an
essential factor in his program; part of it will be spent for main-
tenance on home-leavs; and part of it will have to be put. aside to
provide protection against his own retirement or ill-health:and pro-
tection for his dependents. It is almost a universal objective of
foreigners living in Japan, and other places in the Orient, to retire
eventually and live in the United States.
So, it is very clear that the Japanese standards of income and
living expense are completely irrelevant to theapreigner who is
working in Japan. It is not merely a matter of encouraging the pro-
per kind of foreign executives»namely, men of competence and integrity,
but
RESTRICTED

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
- 2 -
but a matter of avoiding tax measures which will exclude them from
Japan as certainly as though there were an absolute prohibition
against their entrance.
To avoid a tax situation, disadvantageous to the Japanese econo-
my and ruinous to the foreign corporation, a change in the tax laws
is essential so that the rates adopted will be made applicable to
foreign staff resident in Japan only to the extent that their incomes
are drawn in, or remitted to, Japan. Such part of their foreign cur-
rency incomes as are not received in Japan should be free from all
Japanese income and other taxes.
The above proposal is based upon established precedent. As to
foreign business men, resident in the United Kingdom and employed in
the service of a foreign employer, it has been held, under the exist-
ing tax laws and regulations, that the source of their incomes is in.
a "foreign possession" and the incomes are taxable in the United King-
dom only to the extent that they are remitted to the United Kingdom
(see Bennett v. Marshall, (1938) 1 K.B. 591). The inclusion of this
principle in the personal income tax law in Japan might be regarded
as the most important single factor in the revival of Japanese com-
merce and industry in that it permits the investment of American
capital and the aid of American managerial and professional skills.
In fact, it can be said that it would permit American enterprise to
exist in Japan.
II. CORPORATION INCOME TAXES IN JAPAN:
(a). The present regular corporate income tax rate is 35%.
This rate is not such as to encourage corporate enterprise in Japan,
considering the many risks which such enterprise must undergo. Never-
theless, the amount of revenue required is a determining factor and it
may be that something approaching this figure will be required under
present economic and political conditions. There is, however, under
the present tax statutes, an additional excess-profits levy which com-
mences with a 10% levy on profits in excess of 30% of the capital of
the corporation. This excess profits tax, if the revenue is required,
is not too much out of line if, and only if, the measure of "capital"
is realistic.
"Capital", as now determined, is the "average of the amount of
paid up capital, contributions or funds as of the end of each month
of each accounting period, plus the amount of reserves at the begin-
ning date of each account period........."
This definition is not precise and similar definitions in other
foreign countries have proved uncertain and unsatisfactory in admini-
stration. The definition should cover share capital, paid-in or capi-
tal surplus, earned surplus and reserves, and it is of vital importance
that the new legislation clearly and specifically includes each of
these named items of "capital". The same result may be reached in per-
haps a simpler way by defining "capital", for purposes of the tax
statute,
RESTRICTED

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
-3-
statute, as the excess of gross assets over liabilities, though
this latter definition would require specific amplification to avoid
any ambiguity.
(b) The Corporation Tax Law provides, in its Article 15, that
the capital of a corporation, having its head office or principle
place of business outside of Japan, shall be caluulated by applying
the generally applicable provision "to the capital or business located
within the enforcement area of the present law." Presumably, the in-
tent of the law is to determine the total capital of such a corpora-
tion and then to allccate a portion thereof to Japan, the basis being
"capital or business located within the enforcement area." This seems
to be a fair method of allocation but should be more accurately de-
fined by using, for example, the ratio of gross income in Japan to
total gross income. The value would be determined in a foreign cur-
rency and be converted into Japanese currency at the average rate of
exchange ruling in each taxable year, the law or regulations so pro-
viding.
III. PREFECTURAL AND LOCAL TAXES:
It cannot be emphasized too strongly that individual and cor-
porate income taxes are only a part of the tax danger. Even though in-
come taxes are gotten within proper bounds, there remains a ruinous
situation if lccal and provincial taxes are permitted to run their pre-
sent course. A flagrant example is that of the Provincial Business Tax
which is levied on corporations at the rate of 15% of net income. This
is a tax universally adopted throughout Japan and the result is that
corporation income, instead of being taxed at the high rate of 35%, is
taxed at the extraordinary rate of 50%. It is very clear that a total
tax rate upon business income which is above 40% has an inevitable
tendency to contract investments of capital funds in any locality and
the presence in Japan of the many additional risks would add further
discouragement. The Japanese tax-payer, in the past, has worked his
way through the many taxes by presenting false books to the tax
examiner and evading in every conceivable way all or any part of
every tax as opportunity suggested. The reputable foreign business
concern is neither able nor willing to have tvo sets of books or to
indulge in the tax manipulations which tax burdens and tradition have
induced the Japanese to adopt. National, provincial, and local taxes,
in their total burden, at present probably would prevent every corpora-
tion in Japan from earning any net income whatsoever, with consequent
insolvency for every tax-payer who paid in full as the law seems to
require. The Japanese can avoid this dire result by improper practices
but the foreign corporations would not operate under such conditions.
For purposes of certainty, and a proper and honest scheme of taxation,
it would seem, therefore, highly desirable and perhaps essential, that
the national government preempt the entire field of taxation, as ap-
plied to business income, and that local and prefectural governments
be entirely excluded from such fields. While this, at first impres-
sion would seem a drastic limitation upon necessary local and provincial
revenues,
RESTRICTED

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 417,
June 28, 1949.
-4-
revenues, it is very doubtful that this would be the case. A certain
tax, honestly and energetically levied, would result in revenues
hitherto unknown, even at lower rates, and it might well be feasible
for the national government to make returns to the provincial and
local governments out of the total national revenues derived from
taxing business income. This, of course, is a complicated matter,
requiring intensive study, but the objective is so important, from
the standpoint of tax honesty and efficiency, that it is well worth
attempting.
